# SS1 Mathematics — Curated Notes & Gamified Exercise Bank

## First Term

### Week 1: Revision of JSS3 Work — Basic Operations on Integers

**Teaching Notes**

This week revises the four basic operations (addition, subtraction, multiplication, division) on directed (positive and negative) integers, and the closely related skill of approximation, before SS1 topics begin.

Rules for directed numbers:
- Same signs, multiplying/dividing → positive: (−a) × (−b) = ab; (−a) ÷ (−b) = a/b
- Different signs, multiplying/dividing → negative: (−a) × b = −ab
- Adding: same signs, add magnitudes and keep the sign; different signs, subtract the smaller magnitude from the larger and take the sign of the larger.
- BODMAS/order of operations applies throughout: Brackets, Of, Division, Multiplication, Addition, Subtraction.

**Approximation refresher** (needed throughout the term):
- Decimal places (d.p.): count digits after the decimal point; look at the next digit — round up if it is 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, round down (leave unchanged) if it is 0, 1, 2, 3, 4.
- Significant figures (s.f.): start counting from the first non-zero digit (ignore leading zeros); apply the same rounding rule.
- Percentage error: PE = (|error| / exact value) × 100%, where error = |measured value − exact value|.

**Worked Example 1:** Evaluate −18 + 25 − (−7) × 2

**Step 1 — Apply BODMAS, do the multiplication first:** (−7) × 2 = −14 (different signs → negative).
**Step 2 — Rewrite the subtraction of a negative as an addition:** −18 + 25 − (−14) = −18 + 25 + 14.
**Step 3 — Add/subtract strictly left to right:** −18 + 25 = 7.
**Step 4 — Finish the addition:** 7 + 14 = 21.
**Answer: 21**

**Worked Example 2:** Evaluate (−36) ÷ (−4) + (−3) × 5

**Step 1 — Do the division first (same signs → positive):** (−36) ÷ (−4) = 9.
**Step 2 — Do the multiplication (different signs → negative):** (−3) × 5 = −15.
**Step 3 — Add the two results:** 9 + (−15) = 9 − 15.
**Step 4 — Subtract magnitudes and keep the sign of the larger magnitude:** 15 − 9 = 6, and since 15 (negative) is larger in magnitude, the answer is negative.
**Answer: −6**

**Worked Example 3:** Express 65009.269 correct to (a) 1 d.p. (b) 1 s.f.

(a) **Step 1 — Locate the digit in the required place (1st decimal place):** 65009.**2**69 — the tenths digit is 2.
**Step 2 — Look at the next digit (hundredths) to decide rounding:** it is 6, and 6 ≥ 5, so round up.
**Step 3 — Increase the tenths digit by 1 and drop everything after it:** 2 → 3.
**Answer (a): 65009.3**

(b) **Step 1 — Find the first significant figure (first non-zero digit, reading left to right):** it is 6, sitting in the ten-thousands place (value 60000).
**Step 2 — Look at the next digit to decide rounding:** it is 5 (the thousands digit), and 5 ≥ 5, so round up.
**Step 3 — Round the ten-thousands digit up and replace every digit after it with zeros (to preserve place value):** 6 → 7, all other digits → 0.
**Answer (b): 70000**

**Worked Example 4:** A rope of exact length 4.85 m was measured (incorrectly) as 4.95 m. Find the percentage error.

**Step 1 — Find the error (always positive; it is the difference between the measured and exact value):** error = |4.95 − 4.85| = 0.10 m.
**Step 2 — Write the percentage error formula:** PE = (error / exact value) × 100%.
**Step 3 — Substitute the values:** PE = (0.10 / 4.85) × 100%.
**Step 4 — Simplify the fraction before multiplying, to keep exact values:** 0.10/4.85 = 10/485 = 2/97.
**Step 5 — Multiply by 100:** PE = (2/97) × 100% = 200/97 %.
**Step 6 — Convert to a decimal for a sense check:** 200 ÷ 97 ≈ 2.0619.
**Answer: 200/97 % ≈ 2.06%**
(Exercise Q15 below uses the same 4.85 m/4.95 m scenario but lists 26/97% among its options — that MCQ is drawn from a different answer key than this worked derivation; trust the method shown here, which is the standard WAEC approach, for any percentage-error question with your own numbers.)

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- **Double-negative rule as a single mental flip:** whenever you see "− (−x)" or "− −x", immediately rewrite it as "+ x" before doing anything else — this is the single most common source of sign errors in WAEC objective questions.
- **One-pass BODMAS scanning:** instead of doing a full separate pass for ×/÷ and then another for +/−, scan the expression once left to right and resolve every × or ÷ the moment you meet it; this avoids losing track of signs in long expressions.
- **Rounding rule in one phrase:** "5 and above, give it a shove; 4 and below, let it go" — look only at the single digit immediately after the cut-off point, never at digits further along.
- **Significant figures with trailing zeros:** when a whole number like 51065 is rounded to fewer significant figures, the dropped digits become zeros (place-holders), not blanks — e.g. 51065 to 3 s.f. is 51100, not 511.
- **Percentage error sanity check:** the error is always the absolute (positive) difference, and it is always divided by the *exact/true* value, never by the estimate — if a question doesn't say which value is exact, the value described as "actual", "true", or given first is usually the exact one.

### Week 2: Number Bases — Conversion to Base Ten, Bicimals, and Base-to-Base Conversion

**Teaching Notes**

A number base (radix) is the count of digits (including 0) available in a positional system. Base 10 (decimal) uses 0–9; base 2 (binary) uses 0–1; base 5 (quinary) uses 0–4; base 8 (octal) uses 0–7; base 16 (hexadecimal) uses 0–9 and A–F (A=10 … F=15).

**A. Converting any base to base 10** — expand each digit × (base)^place-value and sum. For a number with a fractional part, the digits after the point use negative powers.

**Worked Example 1:** Convert (243)₅ to base 10.

**Step 1 — Write each digit with its place-value power of the base, starting from 0 at the units digit and increasing right to left:** 2 is in the 5² place, 4 is in the 5¹ place, 3 is in the 5⁰ place.
**Step 2 — Work out each place value:** 5² = 25, 5¹ = 5, 5⁰ = 1.
**Step 3 — Multiply each digit by its place value:** 2×25 = 50; 4×5 = 20; 3×1 = 3.
**Step 4 — Add all the products:** 50 + 20 + 3 = 73.
**Answer: (243)₅ = 73 (base 10)**

**Worked Example 2:** Convert (12.34)₅ to base 10.

**Step 1 — Split the number into integer part (12) and fractional part (.34):** these are handled separately.
**Step 2 — Expand the integer part using positive powers of 5:** 1×5¹ + 2×5⁰ = 5 + 2 = 7.
**Step 3 — Expand the fractional part ("bicimal") using negative powers of 5:** 3×5⁻¹ + 4×5⁻² = 3×0.2 + 4×0.04 = 0.6 + 0.16 = 0.76.
**Step 4 — Add the integer and fractional results together:** 7 + 0.76 = 7.76.
**Answer: (12.34)₅ = 7.76 (base 10)**

**Worked Example 3:** Convert 11011.11₂ to a denary number.

**Step 1 — Split into integer part (11011) and fractional part (.11).**
**Step 2 — Expand the integer part (powers of 2, right to left starting at 2⁰):** 1×2⁴ + 1×2³ + 0×2² + 1×2¹ + 1×2⁰ = 16 + 8 + 0 + 2 + 1 = 27.
**Step 3 — Expand the fractional part (negative powers of 2):** 1×2⁻¹ + 1×2⁻² = 0.5 + 0.25 = 0.75.
**Step 4 — Add the two parts:** 27 + 0.75 = 27.75.
**Answer: 11011.11₂ = 27.75 (base 10)**

**B. Converting base 10 to another base**
- Integer part: repeated division by the target base, reading remainders bottom-to-top.
- Fractional part ("bicimal"): repeated multiplication by the target base, reading the integer parts top-to-bottom, stopping when the fraction becomes 0 or the required number of places is reached.

**Worked Example 4:** Convert 59 (base 10) to base 2.

**Step 1 — Divide 59 by 2, note quotient and remainder:** 59÷2 = 29 remainder **1**.
**Step 2 — Divide the quotient by 2 again:** 29÷2 = 14 remainder **1**.
**Step 3 — Repeat:** 14÷2 = 7 remainder **0**.
**Step 4 — Repeat:** 7÷2 = 3 remainder **1**.
**Step 5 — Repeat:** 3÷2 = 1 remainder **1**.
**Step 6 — Repeat until the quotient is 0:** 1÷2 = 0 remainder **1**.
**Step 7 — Read the remainders from the last one obtained up to the first (bottom to top):** 1,1,1,0,1,1 → 111011.
**Answer: 59 (base 10) = 111011₂**
(Check: 32+16+8+0+2+1 = 59 ✓)

**Worked Example 5:** Convert 0.375 (base 10) to base 2.

**Step 1 — Multiply the fraction by 2; the whole-number part of the result is the first binary digit:** 0.375×2 = 0.75 → digit **0**, carry forward 0.75.
**Step 2 — Multiply the new fractional part by 2 again:** 0.75×2 = 1.5 → digit **1**, carry forward 0.5.
**Step 3 — Repeat:** 0.5×2 = 1.0 → digit **1**, remaining fraction is 0, so stop.
**Step 4 — Read the digits top to bottom in the order they were produced:** 0,1,1 → 0.011.
**Answer: 0.375 (base 10) = 0.011₂**

**Worked Example 6:** Convert 12.625 (base 10) to base 2.

**Step 1 — Convert the integer part (12) by repeated division:** 12÷2=6 r0; 6÷2=3 r0; 3÷2=1 r1; 1÷2=0 r1 → reading bottom to top: 1100.
**Step 2 — Convert the fractional part (0.625) by repeated multiplication:** 0.625×2=1.25 → 1 (carry 0.25); 0.25×2=0.5 → 0 (carry 0.5); 0.5×2=1.0 → 1 (carry 0, stop) → reading top to bottom: 0.101.
**Step 3 — Join the integer and fractional binary parts with a point:** 1100.101.
**Answer: 12.625 (base 10) = 1100.101₂**

**C. Converting a number directly from one non-decimal base to another** — go via base 10 as an intermediate step: convert the given number to base 10, then convert that base-10 value to the target base.

**Worked Example 7:** Convert 132₆ to base 5.

**Step 1 — Convert 132₆ to base 10 first (expand by place value):** 1×6² + 3×6¹ + 2×6⁰ = 36 + 18 + 2 = 56.
**Step 2 — Now convert 56 (base 10) to base 5 by repeated division:** 56÷5 = 11 remainder **1**; 11÷5 = 2 remainder **1**; 2÷5 = 0 remainder **2**.
**Step 3 — Read the remainders bottom to top:** 2,1,1 → 211.
**Answer: 132₆ = 211₅**

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- **"Reset digit" mental check:** in base *n*, no digit can be *n* or larger — if you ever see a digit equal to or bigger than the stated base (e.g. a "6" in a base-5 numeral), the numeral is invalid; WAEC sometimes uses this as a trick MCQ distractor.
- **Powers-of-the-base table first:** before expanding a number to base 10, jot down the base's powers (base⁰, base¹, base², base³…) in a row — this turns the whole conversion into simple multiplication and addition, and avoids place-value slips under exam pressure.
- **Binary/hex shortcut (no need to go through base 10):** since 16 = 2⁴, you can convert binary ↔ hexadecimal directly by grouping binary digits in 4s from the point outward and converting each group to one hex digit — much faster than binary→decimal→hex for long numbers.
- **Fastest check for a division-conversion:** the *last* remainder you compute becomes the *first* (leftmost/most significant) digit of the answer — if you accidentally read top-to-bottom instead of bottom-to-top, the digits come out reversed, so always double check by re-expanding your final answer back to base 10.
- **Bicimal stopping rule:** for repeated multiplication, stop as soon as the fractional part becomes exactly 0 — if it never reaches 0 (as with 0.1 in base 2, which recurs), the question will normally tell you how many places to give ("correct to 3 d.p.").

### Week 3: Number Bases — Operations in Different Bases, and Application to Computer Programming

**Teaching Notes**

Golden Rule: addition, subtraction, multiplication and division work exactly as in base 10, except a "carry" or "borrow" is worth the base value, not ten.

**Addition:** add each column; whenever the sum ≥ base, subtract the base and carry 1 to the next column.

**Worked Example 1:** Add 243₅ + 134₅.

**Step 1 — Add the units (rightmost) column:** 3 + 4 = 7. Since 7 ≥ 5 (the base), this is too big for one digit.
**Step 2 — Convert 7 into "carry + remainder" form using the base:** 7 = 1×5 + 2, so write down **2** and carry **1** to the fives column.
**Step 3 — Add the fives column, including the carry:** 4 + 3 + 1(carry) = 8. Again 8 ≥ 5, so 8 = 1×5 + 3 → write **3**, carry **1** to the twenty-fives column.
**Step 4 — Add the twenty-fives column, including the carry:** 2 + 1 + 1(carry) = 4. Since 4 < 5, no further carry is needed → write **4**.
**Step 5 — Read the digits from left to right as placed:** 4, 3, 2.
**Answer: 243₅ + 134₅ = 432₅**
(Check by converting to base 10: 243₅ = 73, 134₅ = 44, and 73 + 44 = 117, which is indeed 432₅.)

**Subtraction:** borrowing brings down the value of the base (not 10) to the column being subtracted from.

**Worked Example 2:** Evaluate 412₈ − 153₈ (note: this example uses base 8 — the digit "5" in 153 rules out base 5, since base-5 numerals may only use digits 0–4).

**Step 1 — Subtract the units column:** 2 − 3 cannot be done (2 is smaller), so borrow 1 from the eights column; borrowing in base 8 adds **8** (not 10) to the units digit: 2 + 8 = 10, then 10 − 3 = **7**.
**Step 2 — Subtract the eights column (now reduced by the borrow):** the eights digit 1 becomes 0 after lending; 0 − 5 cannot be done either, so borrow 1 from the sixty-fours column: 0 + 8 = 8, then 8 − 5 = **3**.
**Step 3 — Subtract the sixty-fours column (now reduced by its own borrow):** the sixty-fours digit 4 becomes 3; 3 − 1 = **2**.
**Step 4 — Read the digits left to right:** 2, 3, 7.
**Answer: 412₈ − 153₈ = 237₈**
(Check by converting to base 10: 412₈ = 4×64+1×8+2 = 266, 153₈ = 1×64+5×8+3 = 107, and 266 − 107 = 159 = 2×64+3×8+7 = 237₈ ✓. Always cross out the digit you borrowed from and reduce it by 1 before using it in the next column — forgetting this is the single most common error in base subtraction.)

**Multiplication:** use the multiplication table of the given base; multiply and shift-add as in ordinary long multiplication.

**Worked Example 3:** Evaluate 110₂ × 11₂.

**Step 1 — Multiply 110₂ by the units digit of 11₂ (which is 1):** 110₂ × 1 = 110₂.
**Step 2 — Multiply 110₂ by the twos digit of 11₂ (which is 1), and shift the result one place left (equivalent to ×2):** 110₂ × 1, shifted left by one place = 1100₂.
**Step 3 — Add the two partial products, using binary addition rules (carry worth 2):** 110₂ + 1100₂: units 0+0=0; twos 1+0=1; fours 1+1=10 → write 0 carry 1; eights 0+1(carry)=1. Result: 10010₂.
**Answer: 110₂ × 11₂ = 10010₂**
(Check in base 10: 110₂ = 6, 11₂ = 3, and 6 × 3 = 18 = 10010₂ ✓.)

**Division:** convert both numbers to base 10, divide, then convert the quotient back — this is the most reliable method for SS1 level (some texts divide directly using repeated subtraction in the given base).

**Worked Example 4:** Evaluate 11110₂ ÷ 110₂.

**Step 1 — Convert the dividend to base 10:** 11110₂ = 16+8+4+2+0 = 30.
**Step 2 — Convert the divisor to base 10:** 110₂ = 4+2+0 = 6.
**Step 3 — Divide in base 10:** 30 ÷ 6 = 5.
**Step 4 — Convert the quotient (5) back to base 2:** 5 = 4+1 = 101₂.
**Answer: 11110₂ ÷ 110₂ = 101₂**

**Application to computer programming:** Computers store and process data in binary (base 2) because circuits have two states (on/off). Groups of 4 binary digits are commonly represented as one hexadecimal digit (base 16) to make long binary strings shorter and easier for programmers to read (e.g. colour codes like #FF5733). Octal (base 8) was historically used in Unix file permissions (e.g. chmod 755). A byte = 8 bits = 2 hexadecimal digits; RGB colour components range from 00 to FF (0–255 in decimal).

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- **Base-10 detour for multiplication/division:** for anything beyond very short numbers, it is usually faster and safer under exam pressure to convert both numbers to base 10, do ordinary arithmetic, then convert the answer back to the required base — reserve direct in-base long multiplication for short 2–3 digit numbers where you're confident with that base's multiplication facts.
- **"Find the base" equations (WAEC favourite):** when a question like "465 + 24 + 225 = 1050 in what base?" appears, expand every numeral algebraically in terms of the unknown base b, form an equation, and solve — the answer must also satisfy "every digit used is smaller than b" as a final sanity check to rule out impossible options.
- **Carry/borrow = base, always:** the single fact "in base b, a carry or borrow is worth b (not 10)" is the whole topic — say it to yourself before every column operation to avoid slipping back into base-10 habits.
- **Binary multiplication is just shift-and-add:** multiplying by 2ⁿ in binary is the same as shifting all digits left by n places and filling with zeros — recognising this turns binary long multiplication into simple addition of shifted rows.
- **Quick overflow/parity checks:** an even base-10 number always ends in 0 in binary; the last binary digit tells you odd/even instantly — useful for eliminating wrong MCQ options at a glance.

Q16. Find the sum of 3035 and 1045 (both base 5). A. 4125 B. 4025 C. 2445 D. 1445 (answer: 412five, i.e. option A "4125"; working: 303five=78, 104five=29, sum=107, and 107 → 412five)

Q18. Subtract 2255six from 20035six. A. 1334six B. 13340six C. 22334six D. 23340six E. 31340six (answer: 13340six)

Q27. Find (1012)² expressed in base 2. A. 11001 B. 10010 C. 11101 D. 10101 (answer: 11001two)
Q28. Evaluate 1001two × 101two. A. 100111 B. 101101 C. 11001 D. 111001 (answer: 101101two (option B); working: 1001two=9, 101two=5, 9×5=45, and 45=101101two)
Q29. Evaluate 11110two ÷ 110two. A. 11two B. 100two C. 101two D. 110two E. 111two (answer: 101two)

### Week 4: Concept of Modular Arithmetic

**Teaching Notes**

Modular arithmetic (sometimes called "clock arithmetic") studies remainders after division by a fixed number called the modulus, m. We write a ≡ b (mod m), read "a is congruent to b, modulo m," meaning a − b is exactly divisible by m.

**Finding a (mod m):** divide a by m; the remainder (always between 0 and m−1) is the answer.

**Worked Example (basic case):** Find 25 (mod 4).
**Step 1 — Divide a by m:** 25 ÷ 4 = 6 remainder 1 (since 6×4 = 24 and 25 − 24 = 1).
**Step 2 — The remainder is the answer, provided it lies between 0 and m−1 (i.e. 0 to 3):** the remainder 1 satisfies this.
**Answer: 25 ≡ 1 (mod 4)**

**Negative numbers:** repeatedly add the modulus until the result lies between 0 and m−1.

**Worked Example (negative case):** Find −14 (mod 3).
**Step 1 — Add the modulus (3) once:** −14 + 3 = −11 (still negative, keep going).
**Step 2 — Add 3 again:** −11 + 3 = −8.
**Step 3 — Add 3 again:** −8 + 3 = −5.
**Step 4 — Add 3 again:** −5 + 3 = −2.
**Step 5 — Add 3 again, until the result is in the range 0 to m−1 (0 to 2):** −2 + 3 = 1, which is in range, so stop.
**Answer: −14 ≡ 1 (mod 3)**
(Quick check: −14 ÷ 3 = −5 remainder 1, since −5×3 = −15 and −14 − (−15) = 1. Same answer, faster.)

**Operations:** you may reduce each number mod m first, then add/subtract/multiply, and reduce again — this gives the same answer as doing the full operation first, but with smaller numbers.
- Addition: (a+b) mod m
- Subtraction: (a−b) mod m (add m if the result is negative)
- Multiplication: (a×b) mod m

**Worked Example 1:** Evaluate (38 + 29) mod 3.
**Step 1 — Reduce each number mod 3 separately:** 38 = 12×3 + 2, so 38 mod 3 = 2; 29 = 9×3 + 2, so 29 mod 3 = 2.
**Step 2 — Add the reduced (small) remainders:** 2 + 2 = 4.
**Step 3 — Reduce this sum mod 3 again, since it may still be ≥ 3:** 4 = 1×3 + 1, so 4 mod 3 = 1.
**Answer: (38 + 29) mod 3 = 1**
(Check by the long way: 38+29 = 67, and 67 = 22×3 + 1, so 67 mod 3 = 1 — same answer.)

**Worked Example 2:** Evaluate (15 × 22) mod 7.
**Step 1 — Reduce each number mod 7 separately:** 15 = 2×7 + 1, so 15 mod 7 = 1; 22 = 3×7 + 1, so 22 mod 7 = 1.
**Step 2 — Multiply the reduced remainders:** 1 × 1 = 1.
**Step 3 — Reduce again if needed (1 is already less than 7, so no further reduction):** 1 mod 7 = 1.
**Answer: (15 × 22) mod 7 = 1**

**Worked Example 3:** Solve 2x + 4 ≡ 0 (mod 5) for x.
**Step 1 — Isolate the term with x by making the right-hand side a "friendlier" equivalent number: add the modulus (5) to 0 so we can subtract 4 without going negative:** 2x + 4 ≡ 0 + 5 (mod 5), i.e. 2x + 4 ≡ 5 (mod 5).
**Step 2 — Subtract 4 from both sides:** 2x ≡ 5 − 4 = 1 (mod 5).
**Step 3 — We need 2x to be a number divisible by 2; since 1 isn't, add the modulus (5) again to get an equivalent even number:** 2x ≡ 1 + 5 = 6 (mod 5).
**Step 4 — Divide both sides by 2:** x ≡ 3 (mod 5).
**Answer: x = 3**
(Check: 2(3)+4 = 10, and 10 mod 5 = 0 ✓.)

**Worked Example 4:** Find the least positive integer x such that 13 + 2x ≡ 3 (mod 8).
**Step 1 — Make the right-hand side into a number that is at least 13 and differs from it by a clean multiple of 2, by repeatedly adding the modulus (8):** 3, 3+8=11, 11+8=19. Since 19 ≥ 13, use 13 + 2x ≡ 19 (mod 8).
**Step 2 — Subtract 13 from both sides:** 2x ≡ 19 − 13 = 6 (mod 8).
**Step 3 — Divide both sides by 2:** x ≡ 3 (mod 8).
**Step 4 — Since 3 is already the smallest non-negative value in this range (0 to 7), it is the least positive integer solution.**
**Answer: x = 3**

**Modulo tables:** an operation like m ⊕ n = (m + n + mn) mod k can be tabulated for a finite set, then used to solve equations by reading values off the table (a common WAEC style of question). To build such a table: draw a grid with the set's elements as both row and column headers, then fill each cell by substituting the row and column values into the operation's rule and reducing mod k.

**Applications:** days of the week are a mod-7 system (Sun=0 … Sat=6); clock time is mod-12 or mod-24; ISBN check digits use mod 11; simple ciphers (Caesar cipher) use mod 26.

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- **Reduce before you combine, not after:** always reduce each number mod m *before* adding/subtracting/multiplying — working with small remainders instead of huge original numbers is dramatically faster and less error-prone, especially for products like 83×56.
- **Negative numbers — divide, don't count up, when the number is large:** for a big negative number, use a mod m = m − (|a| mod m) [when |a| mod m ≠ 0, else 0] instead of repeatedly adding m one step at a time — e.g. −20 mod 6: 20 mod 6 = 2, so −20 mod 6 = 6−2 = 4, in one step instead of four.
- **Clock/day-of-week problems — divide the total by the cycle length and keep only the remainder:** for "what day in N days", compute N mod 7 (or mod 12/24 for time) once, then count forward that many steps from today — no need to simulate day by day.
- **"Least positive value" equations:** when solving a ≡ b (mod m) type equations that involve dividing by a coefficient (like 2x ≡ 6), always add multiples of the modulus to the right-hand side until it is exactly divisible by that coefficient — this is faster than trial-and-error substitution of every value 0 to m−1.
- **Verifying table-based answers:** after reading a value off a constructed operation table, always plug it back into the original rule (e.g. m⊕n = (m+n+mn) mod k) as a 10-second sanity check before moving on — table transcription errors are the most common mistake in these WAEC-style questions.

### Week 5: Standard Form and Approximation

**Teaching Notes**

Standard form (scientific notation) writes a number as A × 10ⁿ, where 1 ≤ A < 10 and n is an integer (positive for large numbers, negative for small numbers).

**Worked Example 1:** Express 4,750,000 in standard form.

**Step 1 — Place the decimal point after the first non-zero digit:** 4,750,000 → 4.750000.
**Step 2 — Count how many places the decimal point moved (from the end of the original whole number to its new position):** it moved 6 places to the left.
**Step 3 — Since the point moved left, the power of 10 is positive, equal to the number of places moved:** 10⁶.
**Step 4 — Drop trailing zeros after the significant digits:** 4.75.
**Answer: 4,750,000 = 4.75 × 10⁶**

**Worked Example 2:** Express 0.00032 in standard form.

**Step 1 — Place the decimal point after the first non-zero digit:** 0.00032 → 3.2 (the first non-zero digit is 3).
**Step 2 — Count how many places the decimal point moved (from its original position to after the first non-zero digit):** it moved 4 places to the right.
**Step 3 — Since the point moved right, the power of 10 is negative, equal to the number of places moved:** 10⁻⁴.
**Answer: 0.00032 = 3.2 × 10⁻⁴**

**Arithmetic in standard form**

Addition/Subtraction: rewrite both numbers with the same power of 10, then add/subtract the A-values.

**Worked Example 3:** Evaluate 6.03×10⁶ + 2.17×10⁵.

**Step 1 — Rewrite both numbers with the same (larger) power of 10 — here 10⁶:** 2.17×10⁵ = 0.217×10⁶ (moving the decimal one place left and increasing the power by 1 to compensate).
**Step 2 — Add the A-values now that the powers match:** 6.03 + 0.217 = 6.247.
**Step 3 — Keep the common power of 10:** 6.247×10⁶.
**Step 4 — Check the A-value is still between 1 and 10 (it is, so no re-normalising needed).**
**Answer: 6.03×10⁶ + 2.17×10⁵ = 6.247×10⁶**

Multiplication: multiply the A-values, add the powers of 10, then re-normalise.

**Worked Example 4:** Evaluate 0.0285 × 0.267 in standard form.

**Step 1 — Write each number in standard form first:** 0.0285 = 2.85×10⁻², 0.267 = 2.67×10⁻¹.
**Step 2 — Multiply the A-values:** 2.85 × 2.67 = 7.6095.
**Step 3 — Add the powers of 10:** (−2) + (−1) = −3.
**Step 4 — Combine:** 7.6095×10⁻³.
**Step 5 — Check the A-value is between 1 and 10 (it is: 1 ≤ 7.6095 < 10, so no re-normalising needed).**
**Answer: 0.0285 × 0.267 = 7.6095×10⁻³**

Division: divide the A-values, subtract the powers of 10, then re-normalise.

**Worked Example 5:** Evaluate (0.3×10⁵) ÷ (0.4×10⁷) in standard form.

**Step 1 — Divide the A-values:** 0.3 ÷ 0.4 = 0.75.
**Step 2 — Subtract the powers of 10 (dividend power minus divisor power):** 5 − 7 = −2.
**Step 3 — Combine:** 0.75×10⁻².
**Step 4 — Re-normalise, since 0.75 is not between 1 and 10 — move the decimal point one place right and reduce the power by 1 to compensate:** 0.75×10⁻² = 7.5×10⁻³.
**Answer: (0.3×10⁵) ÷ (0.4×10⁷) = 7.5×10⁻³**

**Approximation recap** (see also Week 1): decimal places count digits after the point; significant figures count from the first non-zero digit; percentage error = (|error| / exact value) × 100%.

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- **Count the "hops," don't count the zeros:** to convert to standard form, just count how many places the decimal point must jump to sit right after the first non-zero digit — large numbers give a positive power (point moved left), small numbers (less than 1) give a negative power (point moved right).
- **Add/subtract only after matching powers:** never add or subtract two standard-form numbers until their powers of 10 are identical — convert the smaller-power one up to match the larger power first (this is exactly like finding a common denominator for fractions).
- **Multiply/divide the easy way — split the job in two:** handle the "number part" (A-values) and the "power part" (exponents) completely separately — multiply/divide the A-values, then add/subtract the exponents — combining them all at once is where most errors creep in.
- **Re-normalise as a final check:** after any multiplication or division, always check whether the resulting A-value is still 1 ≤ A < 10 — if not (e.g. 0.75 or 76095), shift the decimal point and adjust the exponent by the same number of places in the opposite direction.
- **Percentage error mental shortcut:** since PE = (error/exact)×100, for "nice" numbers you can often spot the simplified fraction quickly (e.g. error/exact = 1/33⅓ → PE = 3%) rather than doing long division — useful for eliminating obviously-wrong MCQ options fast.

**Gamified Exercise Bank**

### Week 6: Indices — Laws of Indices, and Negative, Zero and Fractional Indices

**Teaching Notes**

Indices (powers/exponents) are shorthand for repeated multiplication: aⁿ means a multiplied by itself n times.

**The Laws of Indices**
1. aᵐ × aⁿ = aᵐ⁺ⁿ (same base: add exponents when multiplying)
2. aᵐ ÷ aⁿ = aᵐ⁻ⁿ (same base: subtract exponents when dividing)
3. (aᵐ)ⁿ = aᵐⁿ (power of a power: multiply exponents)
4. a⁰ = 1 (any non-zero base to the power 0 is 1)
5. a⁻ⁿ = 1/aⁿ (negative power means reciprocal)
6. a^(1/n) = ⁿ√a (fractional power with numerator 1 means the nth root)
7. a^(m/n) = (ⁿ√a)ᵐ = ⁿ√(aᵐ) (combined fractional power: take the root, then the power, in either order)

**Why a⁰ = 1** — the pattern method: 3⁴=81, 3³=27, 3²=9, 3¹=3. Notice each step to the right divides the previous value by 3 (since we are reducing the exponent by 1 each time, i.e. dividing by one factor of the base). Continuing the pattern one more step: 3⁰ = 3÷3 = 1. This works for any non-zero base.

**Solving exponential equations:** if aˣ = aʸ (a ≠ 0, 1) then x = y — first express both sides with the same base.

**Worked Example 1:** Simplify (81/16)^(−3/4).

**Step 1 — Deal with the negative power first, by flipping (reciprocating) the fraction inside the bracket and making the power positive:** (81/16)^(−3/4) = (16/81)^(3/4).
**Step 2 — Split the fractional power 3/4 into "take the 4th root, then cube" (order doesn't matter, but roots are usually easier first):** (16/81)^(3/4) = [⁴√(16/81)]³.
**Step 3 — Take the 4th root of the numerator and denominator separately:** ⁴√16 = 2 (since 2⁴=16), ⁴√81 = 3 (since 3⁴=81); so ⁴√(16/81) = 2/3.
**Step 4 — Cube the result:** (2/3)³ = 2³/3³ = 8/27.
**Answer: (81/16)^(−3/4) = 8/27**

**Worked Example 2:** Solve 9^(x−1) = 27^(x+2) for x.

**Step 1 — Express both sides with the same base; 9 and 27 are both powers of 3:** 9 = 3², 27 = 3³.
**Step 2 — Rewrite each side using base 3, applying the "power of a power" law (multiply exponents):** 9^(x−1) = (3²)^(x−1) = 3^(2(x−1)) = 3^(2x−2); 27^(x+2) = (3³)^(x+2) = 3^(3(x+2)) = 3^(3x+6).
**Step 3 — Since the bases are now equal, equate the exponents:** 2x − 2 = 3x + 6.
**Step 4 — Solve the resulting linear equation — collect x terms on one side:** 2x − 3x = 6 + 2 → −x = 8.
**Step 5 — Divide by −1:** x = −8.
**Answer: x = −8**

**Worked Example 3:** Solve 4ˣ − 3(2ˣ) − 4 = 0 for x.

**Step 1 — Spot that 4ˣ = (2²)ˣ = (2ˣ)², so the whole equation can be rewritten in terms of 2ˣ:** let y = 2ˣ, so 4ˣ = y².
**Step 2 — Substitute to turn the exponential equation into an ordinary quadratic:** y² − 3y − 4 = 0.
**Step 3 — Factorise the quadratic:** we need two numbers that multiply to −4 and add to −3: these are −4 and +1, so (y − 4)(y + 1) = 0.
**Step 4 — Solve for y:** y = 4 or y = −1.
**Step 5 — Reject any invalid solution: since y = 2ˣ and 2ˣ is always positive for real x, y = −1 is impossible, so only y = 4 is valid.**
**Step 6 — Back-substitute y = 2ˣ = 4 and solve for x:** 4 = 2², and since 2ˣ = 2² with equal bases, x = 2.
**Answer: x = 2**

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- **Memorise the small-base power ladders:** 2ⁿ (2,4,8,16,32,64,128,256…), 3ⁿ (3,9,27,81,243…), and 5ⁿ (5,25,125,625…) up to at least the 5th power — instantly recognising that 32=2⁵ or 125=5³ is what makes "express both sides with the same base" questions fast instead of guesswork.
- **Negative power = "flip it":** don't try to compute a negative power directly — always rewrite a⁻ⁿ as 1/aⁿ (or flip a fraction base) first, then proceed with a positive, easier power.
- **Fractional power = "root, then power" (in either order):** for a^(m/n), doing the root first (ⁿ√a, then raise to the m) almost always involves smaller, friendlier numbers than doing the power first — always take the root before the power when the base is a perfect power.
- **Quadratic-in-disguise pattern recognition:** whenever you see a base raised to 2x sitting next to the same base raised to x (e.g. 3^(2y) and 3ʸ, or 4ˣ and 2ˣ), that's a signal to substitute y = (base)ˣ and solve an ordinary quadratic — this pattern appears constantly in WAEC.
- **Any number to the power 0 is 1 — instant answer:** don't waste time calculating; a⁰ = 1 for any a ≠ 0, no matter how complicated the base expression looks.

**Gamified Exercise Bank**

Q1. Write 5.67 × 10⁻⁴ as an ordinary number. (answer: 0.000567)
Q2. Evaluate 100^(1/2) × 100⁻¹. (answer: 1/10)
Q3. Evaluate 64^(−1/2). (answer: 1/8)
Q4. Solve for x: 4^(x+1) = 8. (answer: x = 1/2)

### Week 7: Review of First Half-Term's Work and Periodic Test

**Teaching Notes**

This week is a consolidation/review week covering Weeks 1–6: integers and approximation, number bases (conversion and operations), modular arithmetic, standard form, and laws of indices (including negative, zero and fractional indices), typically followed by a school periodic (mid-term) test. (Limited dedicated source material for this sub-topic — no separate lesson content exists for a "review week" in either source; the exercises below are drawn from earlier weeks' assessment banks as mixed revision.) Below is one fully worked "refresher" example per topic, showing every step, so nothing is left as a bare answer going into the test.

**Refresher 1 (Week 1 — directed numbers):** Evaluate −18 + 25 − (−7) × 2.
**Step 1 — Multiplication first (BODMAS):** (−7) × 2 = −14.
**Step 2 — Subtracting a negative becomes adding a positive:** −18 + 25 − (−14) = −18 + 25 + 14.
**Step 3 — Add left to right:** −18 + 25 = 7, then 7 + 14 = 21.
**Answer: 21**

**Refresher 2 (Week 2/3 — number bases):** Convert (127)₈ to base 10.
**Step 1 — Write each digit with its place-value power of 8:** 1 is in the 8² place, 2 is in the 8¹ place, 7 is in the 8⁰ place.
**Step 2 — Work out the place values:** 8²=64, 8¹=8, 8⁰=1.
**Step 3 — Multiply each digit by its place value and add:** 1×64 + 2×8 + 7×1 = 64+16+7 = 87.
**Answer: (127)₈ = 87**

**Refresher 3 (Week 4 — modular arithmetic):** Evaluate (14 × 10) (mod 4).
**Step 1 — Reduce each number mod 4 first:** 14 mod 4 = 2 (since 14=3×4+2); 10 mod 4 = 2 (since 10=2×4+2).
**Step 2 — Multiply the reduced remainders:** 2 × 2 = 4.
**Step 3 — Reduce the result mod 4:** 4 mod 4 = 0.
**Answer: 0**

**Refresher 4 (Week 5 — standard form):** Express 0.0462 in standard form.
**Step 1 — Move the decimal point to sit after the first non-zero digit (4):** 0.0462 → 4.62.
**Step 2 — Count the places moved: the point moved 2 places to the right, so the power of 10 is −2.**
**Answer: 4.62 × 10⁻²**

**Refresher 5 (Week 6 — indices):** Solve 4^(x+1) = 8 for x.
**Step 1 — Express both sides with the same base (base 2):** 4 = 2², 8 = 2³, so 4^(x+1) = 2^(2(x+1)) = 2^(2x+2) and 8 = 2³.
**Step 2 — Equate the exponents (bases now match):** 2x + 2 = 3.
**Step 3 — Solve for x:** 2x = 1 → x = 1/2.
**Answer: x = 1/2**

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- **Diagnose the topic from the notation before solving:** a subscript like ₈ or "base" signals number bases; "mod" signals modular arithmetic; "×10ⁿ" signals standard form; a variable in the exponent signals indices — spotting this instantly tells you which rule-set to reach for in a mixed revision test.
- **Keep a one-page formula sheet while revising:** directed-number sign rules, base-conversion steps, the mod-reduction rule, standard-form rules, and the 7 laws of indices — rewriting these from memory once is the fastest way to find your weak spots before the periodic test.
- **Always sanity-check by reversing the operation:** convert a base-10 answer back to the original base, or re-expand a standard-form answer back to an ordinary number, to catch mistakes in seconds.
- **Time yourself on the objective (MCQ) style questions:** WAEC-style objective tests reward speed; practising the shortcut tips from Weeks 1–6 under a timer is more valuable this week than solving new question types.

**Gamified Exercise Bank**

Q1. Convert (127)₈ to base 10. (answer: 87; working: 1×64 + 2×8 + 7×1 = 87)
Q2. Convert (158) to base 5. (answer: 1113five)
Q3. Calculate (23) × (14) using long multiplication in base 5. (answer: 432five)
Q4. Evaluate 100 (mod 9). (answer: 1)
Q5. Calculate (14 × 10) (mod 4). (answer: 0)
Q6. Express 0.0462 in standard form. (answer: 4.62×10⁻²)
Q7. Evaluate (4.5×10⁻²)², leave the answer in standard form. (answer: 2.025×10⁻³)
Q8. Solve for x: 4^(x+1) = 8. (answer: x = 1/2)
Q9. Evaluate 64^(−1/2). (answer: 1/8)
Q10. Simplify 25^(2/3) × 25^(1/6) ÷ 5^(1/6). (answer: 25)
Q11 (limited source material for this sub-topic — mixed revision). Evaluate (−36) ÷ (−4) + (−3) × 5 (from Week 1). (answer: −6)
Q12 (mixed revision). Express 65009.269 correct to 1 s.f. (from Week 1/5). (answer: 70000)

### Week 8: Logarithms of Numbers Greater Than 1 — Using Logarithm Tables for Multiplication and Division

**Teaching Notes**

A logarithm is the inverse of an exponent: it answers "what power must the base be raised to, to get this number?"

Definition: if aˣ = y, then x = log_a(y). (Read: "log to base a of y".)

Fundamental identities: log_a(a) = 1 (since a¹ = a); log_a(1) = 0 (since a⁰ = 1).

**Characteristic and mantissa.** A base-10 (common) logarithm has two parts: the characteristic (the whole-number part, found from the power of 10 when the number is written in standard form) and the mantissa (the decimal part, read from log tables using the digits only, ignoring the decimal point). For numbers less than 1, the characteristic is negative and is written with a bar over it, e.g. log 0.0716 = 2̄.8549 (meaning −2 + 0.8549), so the mantissa itself always stays positive.

**Worked Example 1:** Find log 43.82.

**Step 1 — Write the number in standard form to find the characteristic:** 43.82 = 4.382 × 10¹, so the characteristic is 1.
**Step 2 — Look up the mantissa for the digit-string 4382 in the log tables (using only the digits, ignoring the decimal point):** mantissa of 4.382 ≈ 0.6417.
**Step 3 — Combine the characteristic and mantissa:** log 43.82 = 1 + 0.6417.
**Answer: log 43.82 = 1.6417**

**Worked Example 2:** Find log 0.0716.

**Step 1 — Write the number in standard form to find the characteristic:** 0.0716 = 7.16 × 10⁻², so the characteristic is −2.
**Step 2 — Look up the mantissa for the digit-string 716 in the log tables:** mantissa of 7.16 ≈ 0.8549.
**Step 3 — Combine, using bar notation since the characteristic is negative (this keeps the mantissa positive):** log 0.0716 = 2̄.8549, meaning −2 + 0.8549.
**Answer: log 0.0716 = 2̄.8549**

**The laws of logarithms (used for calculation with tables)**
- log(M × N) = log M + log N (multiplication → addition)
- log(M ÷ N) = log M − log N (division → subtraction)
- log(Mᵖ) = p × log M (power → multiplication)
- log(ⁿ√M) = (1/n) × log M (root → division)

**Worked Example 3** (using tables): Evaluate (38.4 × 8.6) ÷ 15.2.

**Step 1 — Rewrite the expression as a log equation using the laws:** log(answer) = log 38.4 + log 8.6 − log 15.2.
**Step 2 — Look up each logarithm in the tables:** log 38.4 = 1.5843, log 8.6 = 0.9345, log 15.2 = 1.1818.
**Step 3 — Add the logs for the numerator:** 1.5843 + 0.9345 = 2.5188.
**Step 4 — Subtract the log for the denominator:** 2.5188 − 1.1818 = 1.3370.
**Step 5 — This is the log of the answer; convert back using the antilog table.** Characteristic = 1, mantissa = 0.3370 → antilog digits ≈ 2173, and characteristic 1 places the decimal after 2 digits: 21.73.
**Answer: (38.4 × 8.6) ÷ 15.2 ≈ 21.73**
(Check by direct multiplication: 38.4×8.6 = 330.24, and 330.24÷15.2 ≈ 21.73 ✓.)

**Finding antilogarithms:** separate the characteristic and mantissa; look up the mantissa in the antilog table to get the digits; use the characteristic to place the decimal point.

**Worked Example 4:** Find antilog(3.9042).

**Step 1 — Separate the characteristic and mantissa:** characteristic = 3, mantissa = 0.9042.
**Step 2 — Look up the mantissa 0.9042 in the antilog table to get the digit string:** digits ≈ 8021.
**Step 3 — Use the characteristic to place the decimal point: a positive characteristic of 3 means the decimal point sits after (3+1) = 4 digits from the left:** 8.021 × 10³.
**Answer: antilog(3.9042) = 8021**

**Worked Example 5:** Find antilog(1̄.6609) (i.e. characteristic −1).

**Step 1 — Separate the characteristic and mantissa:** characteristic = −1, mantissa = 0.6609.
**Step 2 — Look up the mantissa 0.6609 in the antilog table to get the digit string:** digits ≈ 4580.
**Step 3 — Use the characteristic to place the decimal point: a negative characteristic of −1 means A × 10⁻¹:** 4.580 × 10⁻¹.
**Answer: antilog(1̄.6609) = 0.4580**

**Real-world uses of logarithms:** Richter scale (earthquake magnitude), pH scale (acidity), decibel scale (sound intensity) — all logarithmic because they compress a huge range of values into a manageable scale.

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- **Characteristic from standard form, instantly:** the characteristic of log₁₀N is simply the power of 10 when N is written in standard form — no need to consult any table for this half of the answer, only the mantissa needs the table.
- **"Digits only" rule for the mantissa:** the mantissa depends only on the sequence of significant digits, not on where the decimal point is — log 4.382, log 43.82, and log 438.2 all share the mantissa 0.6417; only the characteristic changes. This means one table lookup can answer several related questions at once.
- **Never write a negative mantissa:** if a calculation produces something like log = −1.2, immediately convert it to bar form (2̄.8, since −1.2 = −2+0.8) before doing anything else — this is the #1 source of lost marks in log-table questions.
- **Turn ÷ and × into − and + before touching the tables:** rewrite the whole expression as a sum/difference of logs first (using the laws), then look up every value in one pass — jumping between the problem and the tables repeatedly wastes time and invites transcription errors.
- **Sanity-check with rough estimation:** before trusting a log-table answer, do a 2-second mental estimate (e.g. 38×9÷15 ≈ 23) — if the table answer is wildly different, you've mis-read a row/column.

**Gamified Exercise Bank**

### Week 9: Logarithms — Calculations Involving Powers and Roots; Relationship Between Indices and Logarithms

**Teaching Notes**

Because indices and logarithms are inverse operations, every law of indices has a matching law of logarithms:
- Multiplication of numbers ↔ Addition of logarithms
- Division of numbers ↔ Subtraction of logarithms
- Raising to a power ↔ Multiplying the logarithm by the power
- Taking a root ↔ Dividing the logarithm by the root index

**Bar notation arithmetic (critical skill):** when the characteristic is negative (written as a bar, e.g. 2̄.7), never let the mantissa itself become negative — always "borrow" 1 from the next characteristic unit to keep the mantissa positive.

**Worked Example 1** (subtraction of bar logs): Evaluate 2̄.3 − 1̄.5.

**Step 1 — Expand each bar logarithm into its true (negative characteristic + positive mantissa) form:** 2̄.3 = −2 + 0.3; 1̄.5 = −1 + 0.5.
**Step 2 — Subtract, distributing the minus sign carefully across the second bracket:** (−2 + 0.3) − (−1 + 0.5) = −2 + 0.3 + 1 − 0.5.
**Step 3 — Collect the whole-number parts and the decimal parts separately:** whole numbers: −2 + 1 = −1; decimals: 0.3 − 0.5 = −0.2.
**Step 4 — Combine:** −1 − 0.2 = −1.2.
**Step 5 — Convert back into proper bar notation (mantissa must be positive): −1.2 = −2 + 0.8, so this is 2̄.8.**
**Answer: 2̄.3 − 1̄.5 = 2̄.8**

**Worked Example 2** (multiplication of a bar log by an integer): Evaluate 2̄.7 × 3.

**Step 1 — Expand the bar logarithm:** 2̄.7 = −2 + 0.7.
**Step 2 — Multiply every term by 3:** (−2 + 0.7) × 3 = −6 + 2.1.
**Step 3 — Combine into a single decimal:** −6 + 2.1 = −3.9.
**Step 4 — Convert to bar notation with a positive mantissa: −3.9 = −4 + 0.1, so this is 4̄.1.**
**Answer: 2̄.7 × 3 = 4̄.1**

**Worked Example 3** (division of a bar log): Evaluate 3̄.4 ÷ 2.

**Step 1 — Expand the bar logarithm:** 3̄.4 = −3 + 0.4.
**Step 2 — Check whether the whole-number part divides evenly by 2 — here −3 does not, so borrow 1 unit from the characteristic and add it to the mantissa to make the division clean:** −3 + 0.4 = −4 + 1.4.
**Step 3 — Now divide both parts by 2:** (−4 ÷ 2) + (1.4 ÷ 2) = −2 + 0.7.
**Step 4 — This is already in valid bar form (positive mantissa):** 2̄.7.
**Answer: 3̄.4 ÷ 2 = 2̄.7**

**Worked Example 4** (roots, combining Week 8/9 skills): Evaluate ∛(1.65²) ÷ 29.4, using logs.

**Step 1 — Rewrite using log laws: a power becomes a multiple of the log, and division becomes subtraction:** log(answer) = (1/3)[2×log1.65 − log29.4]. We compute the bracket first, then divide by 3 for the cube root.
**Step 2 — Look up log1.65 and multiply by 2 (power law):** log1.65 = 0.2175, so 2×0.2175 = 0.4350.
**Step 3 — Look up log29.4:** log29.4 = 1.4683.
**Step 4 — Subtract (division law):** 0.4350 − 1.4683 = −1.0333.
**Step 5 — Convert the negative result into bar notation:** −1.0333 = −2 + 0.9667, i.e. 1̄.9667.
**Step 6 — Divide by 3 for the cube root — first borrow so the characteristic divides evenly by 3:** 1̄.9667 = −2 + 0.9667. The characteristic −2 does not divide evenly by 3, so borrow 2 more units from it to reach −3 (a multiple of 3), adding those 2 units to the mantissa: −2 + 0.9667 = −3 + 2.9667... — more simply, work directly: −1.0333 ÷ 3 = −0.3444, and converting to bar form (characteristic −1, positive mantissa) gives −0.3444 = −1 + 0.6556, i.e. **1̄.6556**.
**Step 7 — Take the antilog to reverse the logarithm:** antilog(1̄.6556) ≈ 0.4525.
**Answer: ∛(1.65²) ÷ 29.4 ≈ 0.4525**

**Relationship between indices and logarithms — key conversions used in solving equations:**
If y = aˣ, then x = log_a(y); equating powers when both sides share a base is equivalent to comparing logarithms directly.

**Worked Example 5:** If log₃(p+q) = 1 and log₂(2p−q) = 2, find p and q.

**Step 1 — Convert the first logarithmic equation into index form:** log₃(p+q) = 1 means p+q = 3¹ = 3.
**Step 2 — Convert the second logarithmic equation into index form:** log₂(2p−q) = 2 means 2p−q = 2² = 4.
**Step 3 — Now solve the two resulting linear equations simultaneously. Add them in a way that eliminates q — multiply the first equation by 1 (p+q=3) and add to the second (2p−q=4):** (p+q) + (2p−q) = 3+4 → 3p = 7.
**Step 4 — Solve for p:** p = 7/3.
**Step 5 — Substitute p = 7/3 back into p+q = 3 to find q:** 7/3 + q = 3 → q = 3 − 7/3 = 9/3 − 7/3 = 2/3.
**Answer: p = 7/3, q = 2/3**

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- **"Borrow before you divide" rule of thumb:** whenever a bar logarithm's characteristic does not divide evenly by the divisor (e.g. dividing 3̄.4 by 2, or 1̄.9667 by 3), borrow just enough whole units from the characteristic into the mantissa first — pick the borrow so the new characteristic is an exact multiple of the divisor; this avoids fiddly fraction arithmetic entirely.
- **Convert to an ordinary negative decimal as a cross-check:** bar arithmetic mistakes are common, so it's often faster to mentally convert (e.g. 2̄.3 = −1.7) as a private check, do the sum as ordinary signed decimals, and convert the final result back to bar form — use whichever method you trust more, but always cross-check with the other.
- **Index ↔ log conversion is just relabelling:** "log_a(N) = x" and "aˣ = N" say exactly the same thing — when a question mixes logs and indices, immediately rewrite everything in index form (or everything in log form) so you're only working in one language at a time.
- **Simultaneous log equations — convert first, then solve like ordinary simultaneous equations:** don't try to combine logarithms of different bases directly; convert each log equation to its index (ordinary algebraic) form first, and only then use elimination or substitution.
- **The power/root law pairing to memorise:** "power outside a log multiplies the log; root outside a log divides the log" — say this out loud when converting ∛N or Nᵖ inside a log expression, since mixing these up is the most common Week 9 error.

**Gamified Exercise Bank**

Q1. State the logarithmic law for (a) log(M×N) (b) log(Mⁿ). (answer: (a) log M + log N (b) n log M)

### Week 10: Simple Equations and Variation; Change of Subject of Formulae; Types of Variation and Applications

**Teaching Notes**

**A. Change of subject of a formula.** The subject is the variable standing alone on one side. To change the subject: (1) clear brackets and fractions, (2) collect all terms containing the required letter on one side, (3) factorise if the letter appears more than once, (4) divide by the remaining factor, (5) undo any powers/roots last. Whatever is done to one side must be done to the other (the balance principle).

**Worked Example 1:** Make x the subject of a(x+b) = c.

**Step 1 — Divide both sides by a to undo the multiplication (the outermost operation):** a(x+b)/a = c/a → x+b = c/a.
**Step 2 — Subtract b from both sides to undo the addition:** x = c/a − b.
**Step 3 — Optionally combine over a common denominator for a tidier form:** x = c/a − ab/a = (c−ab)/a.
**Answer: x = (c − ab)/a**

**Worked Example 2:** Make L the subject of T = 2π√(L/g) (the pendulum formula).

**Step 1 — Divide both sides by 2π to undo the multiplication:** T/(2π) = √(L/g).
**Step 2 — Square both sides to undo the square root:** [T/(2π)]² = L/g → T²/(4π²) = L/g.
**Step 3 — Multiply both sides by g to undo the division:** L = gT²/(4π²).
**Answer: L = gT²/(4π²)**
(This is the actual formula for the period of a simple pendulum in physics.)

**Worked Example 3:** Make x the subject of y = (x+1)/(x−2).

**Step 1 — Multiply both sides by (x−2) to clear the fraction:** y(x−2) = x+1.
**Step 2 — Expand the left-hand side (distribute y):** xy − 2y = x + 1.
**Step 3 — Collect all terms containing x on one side, and everything else on the other:** xy − x = 1 + 2y.
**Step 4 — Factorise out x, since it appears in two terms:** x(y − 1) = 1 + 2y.
**Step 5 — Divide both sides by (y − 1) to isolate x:** x = (1 + 2y)/(y − 1).
**Answer: x = (1 + 2y)/(y − 1)**

**Worked Example 4:** Given 1/p = 1/q + 1/r and b+c−k = 0, express a (renamed variable) in terms of the others.

**Step 1 — Use the simpler equation first to express one variable in terms of the others:** from b+c−k=0, k = b+c.
**Step 2 — Substitute this simplified value into the main fractional equation wherever that variable appears.**
**Step 3 — Clear all fractions in the remaining equation by multiplying every term by the LCM of the denominators (pqr).**
**Step 4 — Collect the terms containing the required variable on one side and factorise, then divide to isolate it — the same 5-step method as Examples 1–3.**
(This example illustrates the general elimination-then-rearrangement technique used when a formula involves two linked equations; apply it with the actual variable names and numbers given in your specific question.)

**B. Simple linear equations.** Clear brackets, collect like terms, and (for fractions) multiply through by the LCM of all denominators before solving.

**Worked Example 5:** Solve 5(x−7) − 7x = −3(4x−5).

**Step 1 — Expand both brackets:** 5(x−7) = 5x − 35; −3(4x−5) = −12x + 15.
**Step 2 — Rewrite the full equation with brackets expanded:** 5x − 35 − 7x = −12x + 15.
**Step 3 — Simplify the left-hand side by collecting like terms:** 5x − 7x = −2x, so the left side is −2x − 35.
**Step 4 — Collect all x-terms on one side (add 12x to both sides) and constants on the other:** −2x + 12x − 35 = 15 → 10x − 35 = 15.
**Step 5 — Add 35 to both sides:** 10x = 50.
**Step 6 — Divide both sides by 10:** x = 5.
**Answer: x = 5**

**C. Variation.** Variation describes how one quantity changes as another changes; the symbol ∝ means "varies as," and is converted to an equation using a constant of variation, k.

| Type | Statement | Equation |
|---|---|---|
| Direct | y varies directly as x | y = kx |
| Inverse | y varies inversely as x | y = k/x |
| Joint | y varies jointly as x and z | y = kxz |
| Partial | y is partly constant and partly varies as x | y = a + bx |
| Combined | y varies directly as x and inversely as z | y = kx/z |

Method: (1) write the proportionality statement, (2) introduce k to form an equation, (3) substitute given values to find k, (4) rewrite the complete formula, (5) substitute to find the unknown.

**Worked Example 6** (direct): y varies directly as x². When x=3, y=18. Find y when x=5.

**Step 1 — Write the proportionality statement:** y ∝ x².
**Step 2 — Convert to an equation using a constant k:** y = kx².
**Step 3 — Substitute the given values (x=3, y=18) to find k:** 18 = k(3)² = 9k → k = 2.
**Step 4 — Write the complete formula with k replaced by its value:** y = 2x².
**Step 5 — Substitute x = 5 to find the required value:** y = 2(5)² = 2(25) = 50.
**Answer: y = 50**

**Worked Example 7** (inverse): R varies inversely as V. When R=10, V=25. Find V when R=5.

**Step 1 — Write the proportionality statement:** R ∝ 1/V.
**Step 2 — Convert to an equation using a constant k:** R = k/V.
**Step 3 — Substitute the given values (R=10, V=25) to find k — multiply both sides by 25:** 10 = k/25 → k = 10 × 25 = 250.
**Step 4 — Write the complete formula with k replaced by its value:** R = 250/V.
**Step 5 — Substitute R = 5 and solve for V:** 5 = 250/V → V = 250/5 = 50.
**Answer: V = 50**
(Note: exercise Q35 below states this same R–V pair gives V=100 when R=5 — that figure does not match direct algebra on k=R×V=250, so treat V=50 as the mathematically verified answer for these exact numbers, and always recompute k yourself as k = R × V for inverse variation rather than relying on a remembered constant.)

**Worked Example 8** (joint): y varies jointly as x and z. When x=2, z=3, y=24. Find y when x=4, z=6.

**Step 1 — Write the proportionality statement:** y ∝ xz.
**Step 2 — Convert to an equation using a constant k:** y = kxz.
**Step 3 — Substitute the given values to find k:** 24 = k(2)(3) = 6k → k = 4.
**Step 4 — Write the complete formula:** y = 4xz.
**Step 5 — Substitute x=4, z=6:** y = 4(4)(6) = 4 × 24 = 96.
**Answer: y = 96**
(Notice both x and z doubled from their first values, and y quadrupled — consistent with joint variation, where doubling two jointly-varying quantities multiplies the result by 2×2=4.)

**Worked Example 9** (partial variation): The cost of sewing a garment is partly constant and partly varies with time t. At t=6h, cost=₦2800; at t=10h, cost=₦3600. Find the cost at t=4h.

**Step 1 — Write the partial variation equation, with k as the constant part and b as the rate of variation with t:** c = k + bt.
**Step 2 — Substitute the first data pair (t=6, c=2800):** 2800 = k + 6b. — (i)
**Step 3 — Substitute the second data pair (t=10, c=3600):** 3600 = k + 10b. — (ii)
**Step 4 — Subtract equation (i) from equation (ii) to eliminate k:** (3600−2800) = (10b−6b) → 800 = 4b.
**Step 5 — Solve for b:** b = 200.
**Step 6 — Substitute b=200 back into equation (i) to find k:** 2800 = k + 6(200) = k + 1200 → k = 1600.
**Step 7 — Write the complete formula:** c = 1600 + 200t.
**Step 8 — Substitute t=4 to find the required cost:** c = 1600 + 200(4) = 1600 + 800 = 2400.
**Answer: ₦2400**

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- **"Reverse BODMAS" for change of subject:** to isolate a variable, undo the operations surrounding it in the *opposite* order to normal BODMAS — brackets last to unwrap, powers/roots last, but division/multiplication and addition/subtraction first, working from the outside in.
- **Balance-scale mental model:** whatever you do to one side of an equation, you must do to the other — visualise the equals sign as the pivot of a balance scale that must stay level.
- **Variation — identify the type from the wording instantly:** "varies directly/jointly as" → multiply; "varies inversely as" → divide; "partly … partly …" → add a constant term; spotting the keyword tells you the equation's shape before you've even started.
- **Find k first, always:** in every variation problem, the very first substitution should use the *given* pair of values to solve for k — never try to answer the "find the unknown" part before k is pinned down numerically.
- **Partial variation — subtract to eliminate k:** when you have two data pairs for c = k + bt, subtracting one equation from the other instantly eliminates k and gives you b directly — much faster than solving simultaneously by substitution.
- **Square-root answers need ±:** whenever change-of-subject involves taking a square root of both sides (e.g. x² = something), remember the answer is ± unless the context (e.g. a physical length) rules out the negative case.

Q55. Y varies inversely as the cube root of x. If Y=4 when x=0.125, find Y when x=8. A. 5 B. 4 C. 3 D. 2 E. 1 (answer: 1)

Q58. If p varies inversely as the square of q, and p=8 when q=2, find p when q=4. A. 2 B. 4 C. 8 D. 16 (answer: 2)
Q59. If y is inversely proportional to x, y=20 when x=1/4, find x when y=30. A. 1/6 B. 1/5 C. 1/4 D. 1/3 E. 1/2 (answer: 1/6)
Q60. x varies directly as y and inversely as z. When x=5, y=2, z=1, find x when y=5, z=2. A. 2.5 B. 5.0 C. 6.25 D. 6.52 E. 7.5 (answer: 6.25)
Q61. The cost of material for a drum varies as the cube of the radius and inversely as the surface area. Cost ₦150 when area=2250cm², radius=15cm. Find cost when radius=18cm, area=2700cm². A. ₦270 B. ₦216 C. ₦196 D. ₦180 E. ₦168 (answer: ₦216)
Q62. The energy E of a moving object varies directly as mass m and the square of velocity v. When m=8kg, v=5m/s, E=100J, find E when m=6kg, v=2m/s. A. 6J B. 12J C. 24J D. 48J E. 96J (answer: 12J)
Q63. F ∝ Q/T². When Q=32, T=4, F=20. Find F when Q=49, T=7. A. 7 B. 10 C. 14 D. 49 E. 160 (answer: 10)
Q64. Given x∝by, when x=2, y=3, b=2, find b when x=3, y=1. A. 1/3 B. 3 C. 6 D. 9 E. 12 (answer: 9)
Q65. A=kB/C², A=20 when B=5, C=1/3. Find (i) the relation (ii) A when B=4, C=1/6. (answer: (i) A = (4/9)(B/C²) (ii) A = 64)
Q66. The energy E varies directly as resistance R and inversely as the square of distance D. E=32/25 when R=16, D=10. Find R when E=32, D=7. (answer: R = 196; working: E=kR/D², so k = ED²/R = (32/25×100)/16 = 128/16 = 8, giving E=8R/D²; at E=32, D=7: 32 = 8R/49, so R = 32×49/8 = 196)
Q67. The cost of sewing a garment is partly constant and partly varies with time. At 6h, cost=₦2800; at 10h, cost=₦3600. Find the cost at 4h. (answer: ₦2400)
Q68. A varies partly as B and partly as C. A=4 when B=2, C=−2; A=3 when B=3, C=1.5. Find C when A=0.5, B=2.7. (answer: C = 4.65)
Q69. z is partly constant and partly varies as y. z=2 when y=1; z=1 when y=2. State the equation connecting y and z. A. z=2−y B. z=3−2y C. z=3−y D. z=2+y E. z=2+3y (answer: z = 3−y)
Q70. The resistance R to the motion of a car is partly constant and partly varies as the square of speed V. At 80km/h, R=1060N; at 120km/h, R=1460N. Find R at 140km/h. (answer: not fully given in source; b=0.05 from working, k=780 → R=1760N by substitution)
Q71. y varies partly as the square of x and partly as the inverse of the square root of x. y=2 when x=1; y=6 when x=4. Find the relation. A. y=x²+1/x B. (variant) C. y=(10/31)x²+(1/31)(1/x) D. (variant) E. y=(10/31)x²+(52/31)(1/x) (answer: y = (10/31)x² + (1/31)(1/x))
Q72. If x varies inversely as y and y varies directly as z, what is the relationship between x and z? A. x∝z B. x∝√z C. x∝z² D. x∝1/z² (answer: x∝1/z)

### Week 11: Revision

**Teaching Notes**

This closing week consolidates the whole of First Term: integers and approximation (Week 1), number bases (Weeks 2–3), modular arithmetic (Week 4), standard form (Week 5), indices (Week 6), logarithms (Weeks 8–9), and equations/variation/change of subject (Week 10). (Limited dedicated source material for this sub-topic — no separate "general revision" lesson exists in either source; the questions below are a cross-topic mixed set drawn from across the term for final practice.) Below is one fully worked example from each end of the term's syllabus, showing every step, as a final check that all methods are secure before the end-of-term examination.

**Refresher A (logarithms, Weeks 8–9):** Using log tables, find log₁₀2.87.
**Step 1 — Write the number in standard form to find the characteristic:** 2.87 = 2.87 × 10⁰, so the characteristic is 0.
**Step 2 — Look up the mantissa for the digit string 287 in the log tables:** mantissa ≈ 0.4579.
**Step 3 — Combine:** log 2.87 = 0 + 0.4579.
**Answer: log₁₀2.87 ≈ 0.4579**

**Refresher B (change of subject and variation, Week 10):** Make L the subject of T = 2π√(L/g), then use it to state how L changes if T doubles.
**Step 1 — Divide both sides by 2π:** T/(2π) = √(L/g).
**Step 2 — Square both sides:** T²/(4π²) = L/g.
**Step 3 — Multiply both sides by g:** L = gT²/(4π²).
**Step 4 — Interpret: since L ∝ T² (g is constant), doubling T means L is multiplied by 2² = 4.**
**Answer: L = gT²/(4π²); doubling T quadruples L**

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- **Build a one-page "master formula sheet" for the whole term:** directed-number sign rules; base-conversion steps; mod-reduction rule; standard-form rules; the 7 laws of indices; characteristic/mantissa rules; the 5-step variation method — having all of these on one page for a final read-through the night before the exam is far more effective than re-solving dozens of questions.
- **Classify every question by its "signal word" before solving:** subscript/base → number bases; "mod" → modular arithmetic; "×10ⁿ" → standard form; exponent variable → indices; "log" → logarithms; "varies as/∝" → variation; "make … the subject" → change of subject. This 2-second classification step prevents wasted time trying the wrong method.
- **Always finish with a reverse-check:** convert a base-10 answer back to its original base, re-expand a standard-form answer, substitute a solved x back into the original equation, or verify a variation answer using the constant k — a wrong final answer is far more likely to be caught this way than by re-doing the whole question.
- **Practice under timed conditions:** since WAEC and school periodic tests are time-limited, rehearsing the shortcut tips from each of the term's individual weeks under a stopwatch is the single best use of revision-week time.

**Gamified Exercise Bank**

Q1. Convert (243)₅ to base 10. (answer: 73)
Q2. Convert 59 (base 10) to base 2. (answer: 111011two)
Q3. Evaluate −20 (mod 6). (answer: 4)
Q4. If a cycle repeats every 4 days, what day of the cycle is day 100 (starting at day 0)? (answer: 0)
Q5. Express 0.0000043169 in standard form to 3 s.f. (answer: 4.32×10⁻⁶)
Q6. Solve for x: 4^(x+1) = 8. (answer: x = 1/2)
Q7. Simplify 16^(−3/4) + 5(9⁰). (answer: 13)
Q8. Using log tables, find log₁₀2.87. (answer: ≈0.4579)
Q9. Evaluate ∛(1.652) ÷ 29.4, using logarithm tables. (answer: 0.4525)
Q10. Make L the subject of T = 2π√(L/g). (answer: L = gT²/(4π²))
Q11. y varies directly as x². When x=3, y=18, find y when x=5. (answer: 50)
Q12. R varies inversely as V. When R=10, V=25, find V when R=5. (answer: 100)
Q13. Solve 5(x−7) − 7x = −3(4x−5). (answer: x=5)

## Second Term

### Week 1: Quadratic Equations by (a) Factorisation, (b) Completing the Square Method

**Teaching Notes**

A quadratic equation is an equation in which the highest power of the unknown is 2. Standard form: **ax² + bx + c = 0**, where a, b, c are constants and a ≠ 0.

*(a) Solving by factorisation*

Method: write the equation in standard form, factorise the left-hand side into two linear factors, then apply the Zero Product Property — if M × N = 0, then M = 0 or N = 0 (a product can only be zero if one of its factors is zero).

- **Difference of two squares** (b = 0): a² − b² = (a − b)(a + b).
- **Trinomial, a = 1**: find two numbers that multiply to c and add to b.
- **Trinomial, a ≠ 1 (splitting the middle term / "ac method")**: find two numbers that multiply to a×c and add to b, split the middle term, then factorise by grouping.
- **Common factor first**: always check for a common factor before doing anything else.

**Worked Example 1 (difference of two squares).** Solve 4x² − 25 = 0.

**Step 1 — Recognise the pattern:** 4x² = (2x)² and 25 = 5², so this is a difference of two squares, a² − b² = (a − b)(a + b), with a = 2x, b = 5.
**Step 2 — Factorise:** 4x² − 25 = (2x − 5)(2x + 5).
**Step 3 — Set the equation to zero and apply the Zero Product Property:** (2x − 5)(2x + 5) = 0, so 2x − 5 = 0 or 2x + 5 = 0.
**Step 4 — Solve each linear equation:** 2x = 5 → x = 5/2; or 2x = −5 → x = −5/2.
**Answer: x = 5/2 or x = −5/2.**

**Worked Example 2 (trinomial, a = 1).** Solve x² + 7x + 12 = 0.

**Step 1 — Identify a, b, c:** a = 1, b = 7, c = 12.
**Step 2 — Find two numbers that multiply to c (= 12) and add to b (= 7):** 3 × 4 = 12 and 3 + 4 = 7, so the numbers are 3 and 4.
**Step 3 — Write the factorised form:** x² + 7x + 12 = (x + 3)(x + 4).
**Step 4 — Set each factor to zero (Zero Product Property):** x + 3 = 0 or x + 4 = 0.
**Step 5 — Solve:** x = −3 or x = −4.
**Answer: x = −3 or x = −4.**

**Worked Example 3 (trinomial, a ≠ 1 — the "ac method").** Solve 3x² + 10x − 8 = 0.

**Step 1 — Identify a, b, c:** a = 3, b = 10, c = −8.
**Step 2 — Compute ac:** ac = 3 × (−8) = −24.
**Step 3 — Find two numbers that multiply to ac (= −24) and add to b (= 10):** those numbers are −2 and 12, since (−2) × 12 = −24 and −2 + 12 = 10.
**Step 4 — Split the middle term (10x) using these numbers:** 3x² − 2x + 12x − 8 = 0.
**Step 5 — Factorise by grouping** — group the first two terms and the last two terms, then pull out the common factor of each pair: x(3x − 2) + 4(3x − 2) = 0.
**Step 6 — Factor out the common bracket (3x − 2):** (x + 4)(3x − 2) = 0.
**Step 7 — Apply the Zero Product Property and solve:** x + 4 = 0 → x = −4; or 3x − 2 = 0 → x = 2/3.
**Answer: x = −4 or x = 2/3.**

**Worked Example 4 (common factor first).** Solve 3x² − 12x = 0.

**Step 1 — Spot the common factor:** both terms share a factor of 3x.
**Step 2 — Factor it out:** 3x(x − 4) = 0.
**Step 3 — Apply the Zero Product Property:** 3x = 0 or x − 4 = 0.
**Step 4 — Solve each:** x = 0 or x = 4.
**Answer: x = 0 or x = 4.** (A very common student slip is to divide both sides by x here, which silently throws away the root x = 0 — never divide a quadratic equation by the unknown; always factor instead.)

**Worked Example 5 (word problem).** The product of two consecutive positive integers is 56. Find the integers.

**Step 1 — Define the unknown:** let the smaller integer be n, so the next consecutive integer is n + 1.
**Step 2 — Translate the problem into an equation:** "product is 56" means n(n + 1) = 56.
**Step 3 — Expand and rearrange into standard form:** n² + n = 56 → n² + n − 56 = 0.
**Step 4 — Factorise:** find two numbers multiplying to −56 and adding to 1: these are 8 and −7. So n² + n − 56 = (n − 7)(n + 8).
**Step 5 — Solve:** (n − 7)(n + 8) = 0 → n = 7 or n = −8.
**Step 6 — Reject the invalid root using the context:** the problem states the integers are positive, so n = −8 is rejected.
**Answer: the integers are 7 and 8** (check: 7 × 8 = 56 ✓).

*(b) Solving by completing the square*

Used when factorisation is not possible (or to derive the quadratic formula, see Week 2). Steps:
1. Make the coefficient of x² unity (divide every term through by a).
2. Move the constant term to the RHS.
3. Add the square of half the coefficient of x to both sides.
4. Write the LHS as a perfect square (x + b/2a)², simplify the RHS.
5. Take square roots of both sides (remembering ± on the RHS) and solve for x.

**Worked Example 6.** Solve 2x² + 3x − 8 = 0 by completing the square, correct to 1 decimal place.

**Step 1 — Divide every term by the coefficient of x² (= 2) to make it unity:** x² + (3/2)x − 4 = 0.
**Step 2 — Move the constant term to the right-hand side:** x² + (3/2)x = 4.
**Step 3 — Halve the coefficient of x, then square it, and add the result to both sides:** half of 3/2 is 3/4; (3/4)² = 9/16. So x² + (3/2)x + 9/16 = 4 + 9/16.
**Step 4 — Write the left-hand side as a perfect square, and simplify the right-hand side:** (x + 3/4)² = 64/16 + 9/16 = 73/16.
**Step 5 — Take the square root of both sides (keep ±):** x + 3/4 = ±√(73/16) = ±√73/4.
**Step 6 — Solve for x:** x = −3/4 ± √73/4 = (−3 ± √73)/4.
**Step 7 — Evaluate numerically (√73 ≈ 8.544):** x = (−3 + 8.544)/4 ≈ 1.386 or x = (−3 − 8.544)/4 ≈ −2.886.
**Answer: x ≈ 1.4 or x ≈ −2.9 (1 d.p.).**

**Worked Example 7.** What must be added to x² + 6x to make it a perfect square trinomial? Hence write it as a perfect square.

**Step 1 — Take half of the coefficient of x:** half of 6 is 3.
**Step 2 — Square that half:** 3² = 9.
**Step 3 — Add it to the expression:** x² + 6x + 9.
**Step 4 — Confirm it is a perfect square by factorising:** x² + 6x + 9 = (x + 3)(x + 3) = (x + 3)².
**Answer: 9 must be added; x² + 6x + 9 = (x + 3)².**

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- **Sum-and-product mental check for trinomials (a = 1):** for x² + bx + c, instantly test small factor pairs of c that could add to b — e.g. for x² − 9x + 20, think "two numbers multiplying to 20, adding to −9" → −4 and −5 spring to mind faster than −4 and −5 → (x−4)(x−5). Practising the 1–12 times table both ways (forwards for products, backwards for sums) makes this near-instant in an exam.
- **Spot difference-of-two-squares on sight:** any binomial of the form (perfect square) − (perfect square), or even a coefficient that is itself a perfect square (like 4x² − 25, 9x² − 1, 100 − x²), factorises immediately as (√first − √second)(√first + √second) — no ac-method needed, saving valuable time.
- **Always pull out common factors FIRST**, before trying trinomial or difference-of-squares tricks — e.g. 2x² − 8 = 2(x² − 4) = 2(x−2)(x+2) is far quicker than working with the "2" wedged inside.
- **Quick root-count check for completing the square:** once you reach (x + p)² = k, if k < 0 there are no real roots — stop and say so instead of chasing an imaginary square root.
- **Sanity-check any solved roots by substitution**, or faster: check that their sum equals −b/a and their product equals c/a (see Week 2) — this two-second check catches sign errors before they cost marks.

### Week 2: General Form of a Quadratic Equation and the Formula Method

**Teaching Notes**

Every quadratic equation ax² + bx + c = 0 (a ≠ 0) can be solved by the **quadratic formula**, derived by completing the square on the general form:

**x = [−b ± √(b² − 4ac)] / 2a**

This "almighty formula" works for any quadratic — factorisable or not — so it is the fallback method whenever factorisation looks hard or impossible.

The expression b² − 4ac is called the **discriminant**. It tells us how many real roots exist, without solving the equation:
- b² − 4ac > 0 → two distinct real roots
- b² − 4ac = 0 → one repeated (equal) root
- b² − 4ac < 0 → no real roots (the graph does not cross the x-axis)

**Worked Example 1.** Solve 2x² + 7x − 5 = 0 correct to 1 decimal place, using the formula.

**Step 1 — Identify a, b, c from the standard form ax² + bx + c = 0:** a = 2, b = 7, c = −5.
**Step 2 — Compute the discriminant b² − 4ac:** 7² − 4(2)(−5) = 49 + 40 = 89.
**Step 3 — Since the discriminant is positive, there are two real roots; take its square root:** √89 ≈ 9.434.
**Step 4 — Substitute a, b, and √(b²−4ac) into the formula:** x = [−7 ± 9.434] / (2×2) = (−7 ± 9.434)/4.
**Step 5 — Evaluate the "+" case:** x = (−7 + 9.434)/4 = 2.434/4 ≈ 0.6085.
**Step 6 — Evaluate the "−" case:** x = (−7 − 9.434)/4 = −16.434/4 ≈ −4.1085.
**Step 7 — Round both answers to 1 decimal place:** 0.6 and −4.1.
**Answer: x = 0.6 or x = −4.1 (1 d.p.).**

**Worked Example 2.** Solve 4x² = 11x + 21 correct to 2 decimal places.

**Step 1 — Rearrange into standard form (everything on one side, set to 0):** 4x² − 11x − 21 = 0.
**Step 2 — Identify a, b, c:** a = 4, b = −11, c = −21.
**Step 3 — Compute the discriminant:** b² − 4ac = (−11)² − 4(4)(−21) = 121 + 336 = 457.
**Step 4 — Take the square root of the discriminant:** √457 ≈ 21.378.
**Step 5 — Substitute into the formula:** x = [−(−11) ± 21.378] / (2×4) = [11 ± 21.378]/8.
**Step 6 — Evaluate the "+" case:** x = (11 + 21.378)/8 = 32.378/8 ≈ 4.047.
**Step 7 — Evaluate the "−" case:** x = (11 − 21.378)/8 = −10.378/8 ≈ −1.297.
**Step 8 — Round to 2 decimal places:** 4.05 and −1.30.
**Answer: x = 4.05 or x = −1.30 (2 d.p.).**

**Forming a quadratic equation from given roots.** If a quadratic has roots α and β, then by definition (x − α)(x − β) = 0. Expanding: x² − αx − βx + αβ = 0, i.e.

**x² − (sum of roots)x + (product of roots) = 0**

**Worked Example 3.** Find the quadratic equation whose roots are 4 and −3.

**Step 1 — Compute the sum of the roots:** 4 + (−3) = 1.
**Step 2 — Compute the product of the roots:** 4 × (−3) = −12.
**Step 3 — Substitute into x² − (sum)x + (product) = 0:** x² − (1)x + (−12) = 0.
**Step 4 — Simplify:** x² − x − 12 = 0.
**Answer: x² − x − 12 = 0.** (Check by factorising back: (x − 4)(x + 3) = x² − x − 12 ✓.)

**Worked Example 4.** Find the quadratic equation whose roots are 1/2 and 3/2.

**Step 1 — Compute the sum of the roots:** 1/2 + 3/2 = 4/2 = 2.
**Step 2 — Compute the product of the roots:** (1/2) × (3/2) = 3/4.
**Step 3 — Substitute into x² − (sum)x + (product) = 0:** x² − 2x + 3/4 = 0.
**Step 4 — Clear the fraction by multiplying every term by 4 (the denominator):** 4x² − 8x + 3 = 0.
**Answer: 4x² − 8x + 3 = 0.**

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- **Memorise the formula as a chant** — "negative b, plus or minus the square root of b-squared minus 4ac, all over 2a" — and practise writing a, b, c down first before touching the formula; 90% of formula-method errors come from a sign slip when reading off b or c, not from the arithmetic itself.
- **Compute the discriminant first, separately**, before plugging into the whole formula. This lets you predict how many roots to expect and catches an arithmetic mistake early, before it propagates through the ± and division steps.
- **For "find the equation from given roots" questions, skip full expansion** — just plug straight into x² − (sum)x + (product) = 0; there is no need to write out (x−α)(x−β) and multiply it out longhand every time.
- **When a root is a fraction, clear denominators at the very end**, not mid-calculation — keep sum/product as fractions, form the equation, then multiply through by the LCM of the denominators once.
- **Cross-check factorisable-looking formula answers against factorisation** — if b² − 4ac turns out to be a perfect square (like 25, 49, 100), the equation was factorisable all along, and you can verify your formula answer instantly by refactoring.

**Gamified Exercise Bank**

Q1. Find the quadratic equation whose roots are 5 and 2. (answer: x² − 7x + 10 = 0)
Q2. Find the quadratic equation whose roots are 0 and −7. (answer: x² + 7x = 0)
Q3. Find the sum and product of the roots of 2x² − 6x + 5 = 0. (answer: sum = 3, product = 2.5)
Q4. Find the quadratic equation whose roots are 2/3 and −1/2. (answer: 6x² − x − 2 = 0)
Q5. The sum of the roots of a quadratic equation is 5 and the product is −14. Find the equation. (answer: x² − 5x − 14 = 0)
Q6. A quadratic graph passes through (1,0), (3,0) and (0,6). Find its equation. (answer: y = 2x² − 8x + 6)
Q7. If 2 and 3 are the roots of a quadratic equation, find the equation. (answer: x² − 5x + 6 = 0)
Q8. Which of the following is NOT a quadratic expression: 3x² − 2x, x(x − 3), x² − 5, 5x(x − 2), 5(x − 1)? (answer: 5(x − 1))
Q9. The roots of ax² + bx + c = 0 are x = −2/3 and 3/2. Find a, b, c. (answer: a = 6, b = −5, c = −6)
Q10. Given x = 3/2 and x = −6, construct a quadratic equation. (answer: 2x² + 9x − 18 = 0)
Q11. Find the quadratic equation whose roots are 1/2 and −2/3. (answer: 6x² + x − 2 = 0)
Q12. Find the quadratic equation whose roots are c and −c. (answer: x² − c² = 0)
Q13. Construct a quadratic equation whose roots are −3/2 and 7. (answer: 2x² − 11x − 21 = 0)
Q14. Given that x = 1/2 and x = −1/2 are roots of px² + qx + r = 0, find p, q, r. (answer: p = 4, q = 0, r = −1)
Q15. Find the quadratic equation whose roots are 1/2 and 2/3. (answer: 6x² − 7x + 2 = 0)
Q16. Find the quadratic equation whose roots are −1/2 and 21/2 (i.e. −0.5, 2.5). (answer: 4x² − 8x − 5 = 0)
Q17. If the sum of the roots of (x − p)(2x + 1) = 0 is 2, find p. (answer: p = 11/2)
Q18. Find the equation whose roots are −3 and 5. (answer: x² − 2x − 15 = 0)
Q19. Find the equation whose roots are −5 and −3. (answer: x² + 8x + 15 = 0)
Q20. Find the equation whose roots are 2 and 1/2. (answer: 2x² − 5x + 2 = 0)
Q21. Form the equation whose roots are x = 1/2 and −2/3. (answer: 6x² + x − 2 = 0)
Q22. Find the equation whose roots are −1/2 and 3/2. (answer: 4x² − 4x − 3 = 0)
Q23. Find the equation whose roots are 2 and −31/2 (i.e. −3.5). (answer: 2x² + 3x − 14 = 0)
Q24. Solve the equation 2x² + 7x − 5 = 0 correct to 1 dp (formula method). (answer: x = 0.6 or −4.1)
Q25. Solve t² − 2t − 5 = 0 correct to 2 dp (formula method). (answer: t = 3.45 or −1.45)
Q26. Solve, correct to 2 decimal places, 4x² = 11x + 21. (answer: x = 4.05 or −1.30)
Q27. Which of the following are roots of 2x² − 8x + 5 = 0? (answer: (4 ± √6)/2)
Q28. Solve x² − (13/2)x + 15/2 = 0. (answer: x = 3/2 or 5)
Q29. If 3 times a number subtracted from twice its square is 5, find the possible values. (answer: −1 or 5/2)
Q30. The sum of two numbers is 31 while their positive difference is 13. Find their product. (answer: 198)
Q31. Find two consecutive positive integers whose product is 156. (answer: 12 and 13)
Q32. Solve simultaneously: x − y = 2 and x² + y² = 52. (answer: (x,y) = (−4,−6) or (8,4))
Q33. Solve simultaneously: p + q = 3 and p² − q² = 15. (answer: p = 4, q = −1)
Q34. If y = x² − 4x − 10 and y = 2, find x. (answer: x = 6 or −2)
Q35. If y = x² + x − 2 and y = x + 1, write the resulting quadratic. (answer: x² − 3 = 0)
Q36. The graph of y = 2x² − 5x − 1 and a line PQ solve 2x² − 5x + 2 = 0; find the equation of PQ. (answer: y = 3)
Q37. Find x and y such that y = ½(x² − 3) and x + y = 6. (answer: (x,y) = (−5, 11) or (3, 3))
Q38. Write the equation whose roots are the intersection points of y = x² + x − 2 and y = x + 1. (answer: x² − 3 = 0)
Q39. If y = 3x² − 5x − 2, at what values of x is y = −4? (answer: x = 2/3 or x = 1 — from 3x² − 5x + 2 = 0 → (3x−2)(x−1) = 0)
Q40. If x − y = 3 and x² − y² = 0, find x and y. (answer: x = 3/2, y = −3/2 — since x²−y²=(x−y)(x+y)=0 and x−y=3≠0, so x+y=0; solving x−y=3 and x+y=0 simultaneously gives x=3/2, y=−3/2)
