### Week 7: Construction of Quadrilaterals and the Equilateral Triangle

**Teaching Notes**

A **quadrilateral** (four-sided figure) is generally not "rigid" like a triangle — you typically need **5 independent pieces of information** to construct one uniquely (compared to 3 for a triangle). The standard method is to split the quadrilateral into two triangles using a diagonal, and construct each triangle in turn.

**Equilateral triangle:** all three sides equal, all three angles = 60°. To construct one of side length s: draw a base of length s; at each end, construct a 60° angle (or, equivalently, draw two arcs of radius s centred at each end of the base — their intersection is the third vertex).

**Constructing a parallelogram ABCD** given AB, BC, and ∠ABC: draw AB; at B construct the given angle; mark BC along the new ray; then find D either (a) by compass arcs — from A with radius BC, and from C with radius AB, intersecting at D — or (b) by copying ∠ABC at A (co-interior/alternate angle reasoning for parallel sides) and marking off the length.

**Properties used to check a parallelogram construction:** opposite sides equal and parallel; opposite angles equal; adjacent angles supplementary (sum to 180°); diagonals bisect each other.

**Trapezium:** one pair of parallel sides; constructed by drawing a base, constructing the given angle, drawing a parallel line at the specified perpendicular distance (or through a marked point), and completing the sides.

**Rhombus:** a parallelogram with all four sides equal; constructed like a parallelogram but with BC = AB.

**Constructing a parallel line through a point outside a given line:** with the point C given and line AB, draw an arc from C with radius AB cutting near the line; then with the same original AB-arc technique (equal-radius arcs from A and C), find the fourth point D so that CD ∥ AB.

**Worked example:** Construct parallelogram ABCD with AB = 6 cm, BC = 4 cm, ∠ABC = 60°.
- **Step 1 — Draw the base:** using a ruler, draw AB = 6 cm exactly.
- **Step 2 — Construct 60° at B:** compass on B, arc cutting BA at a point; same radius, compass on that point, second arc crossing the first; the ray from B through that crossing point makes ∠ABC = 60° with BA.
- **Step 3 — Mark off BC along the new ray:** measure 4 cm from B along this 60° ray with the ruler, and label the point C.
- **Step 4 — Locate D using the "opposite sides equal" property of a parallelogram (AD must equal BC = 4 cm, and CD must equal AB = 6 cm):** open the compass to 4 cm, place the point on A, and draw an arc on the side of AB opposite to where you'd expect the shape to fold outward (roughly above the figure, same side as C).
- **Step 5 — Draw the second locating arc:** open the compass to 6 cm, place the point on C, and draw an arc that crosses the arc from Step 4.
- **Step 6 — Mark the crossing point as D.**
- **Step 7 — Join the sides:** draw AD and CD with a ruler to complete the parallelogram.
- **Step 8 — Verify:** AD should measure 4 cm (matching BC) and CD should measure 6 cm (matching AB); ∠BCD should measure 180° − 60° = 120° (co-interior with ∠ABC across the parallel sides AB and DC).
- **Answer: parallelogram ABCD is constructed with AB = DC = 6 cm, BC = AD = 4 cm, ∠ABC = ∠ADC = 60°, and ∠BCD = ∠DAB = 120°.**

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- For any parallelogram construction, once you have two sides and the included angle, D is *always* found by the same "double-arc" trick: swing an arc from A with radius = the second given side, and from C (or the far end of the second side) with radius = the first given side — their crossing point is D every time, with no need for parallel-line construction.
- Remember the shortcut for a rhombus: it's just a parallelogram construction where the *second* side length is made equal to the first, so you can reuse the exact same steps as a parallelogram, only changing one measurement.
- For quadrilaterals in general, always split into two triangles with one diagonal (e.g. AC or BD) and construct SSS or SAS triangle by triangle — trying to place all four vertices "by eye" at once is the fastest way to end up with an inaccurate shape.
- Check a finished parallelogram fast, without re-measuring every side: its diagonals must bisect each other, so if you draw both diagonals, they should cross at the exact midpoint of each — a quick way to catch a construction error.
- When two "opposite" angles are asked for as a check (e.g. verify ∠BCD after constructing ∠ABC), use co-interior angles (they sum to 180° across parallel sides) instead of a protractor — it's faster and shows your geometric reasoning to an examiner.

**Gamified Exercise Bank**

Q1. How many pieces of information are generally needed to construct a unique quadrilateral? (answer: 5)
Q2. What is the standard method for constructing a quadrilateral? (answer: split it into two triangles using a diagonal, and construct each triangle)
Q3. List the four properties of a parallelogram used to verify a construction. (answer: opposite sides equal & parallel; opposite angles equal; adjacent angles supplementary; diagonals bisect each other)
Q4. Construct a parallelogram ABCD with AB = 6 cm, BC = 4 cm, ∠ABC = 60°. (answer: construction task)
Q5. Construct a rhombus ABCD with side 5 cm and ∠A = 60°. (answer: construction task)
Q6. Using a ruler and compasses only, construct a rhombus PQRS of side 7 cm with ∠PQR = 60°. Locate point X equidistant from PQ and QR, and also equidistant from Q and R; measure XR. (answer: construction task)
Q7. Construct parallelogram PQRS with RS as base, PQ = 7.8 cm, QR = 5.6 cm, ∠QRS = 120°; then construct rectangle ABRS equal in area to PQRS. Measure AP and AS. (answer: construction task)
Q8. Construct parallelogram EFGH with EF = 9 cm, EG = 11.5 cm, ∠EFG = 105°. Construct a circle through E, F, G; measure FH and the circle's radius; find the parallelogram's area. (answer: construction task, area computable once base/height are measured)
Q9. Construct a trapezium WXYZ with WX = 8 cm, XY = 5.5 cm, XZ = 8.3 cm, ∠WXY = 60°, and WX ∥ ZY; then construct rectangle PQYZ with P, Q on WX. Measure QX and ∠XWZ. (answer: construction task)
Q10. Construct a trapezium PQRS with parallel sides PQ and SR 5 cm apart, ∠SPQ = 60°, PQ = 9 cm, SR = 6 cm. Construct the locus l₁ of points equidistant from P and Q, and l₂ of points 7 cm from P; locate M₁ and M₂ where they meet these loci, and measure M₁M₂. (answer: M₁M₂ = 10.8 cm)
Q11. Construct a trapezium ABCD with parallel sides AB and DC 2 cm apart, ∠DAB = 60°, AB = 4 cm, BC = 2.5 cm. Measure the possible length(s) of DC to the nearest cm. (answer: construction task)
Q12. Using ruler and compasses, construct quadrilateral PQRS with ∠PQR = 75°, ∠QRS = 60°, PQ = 6 cm, QR = 8 cm, PS ∥ QR. Measure RS. Construct the locus l₁ of points equidistant from Q and R, and l₂ of points equidistant from QR and RS; locate T where they meet and measure PT. (answer: construction task)
Q13. Construct △ABC with AB = 9 cm, ∠A = 60°, and locate D on BC (using a suitable parallel construction) such that CD ∥ AB. Construct a quadrilateral incorporating this parallel line. (answer: construction task)
Q14. What distinguishes a trapezium construction from a parallelogram construction in terms of given information? (answer: a trapezium needs the two (different-length) parallel sides plus their separation and an angle, since only one pair of sides is parallel; a parallelogram's two pairs of opposite sides are automatically equal and parallel once three key measurements — two sides and the included angle — are fixed)
Q15. Construct an equilateral triangle of side 6 cm using arcs only (no protractor). (answer: construction task — draw base 6 cm, then arcs of radius 6 cm from each end meeting at the apex)
Q16. Why does drawing two equal-radius arcs from the two ends of a base segment produce an equilateral triangle? (answer: both new sides equal the radius used, which is also set equal to the base length, so all three sides are equal)
Q17. Construct a rectangle PQRS with PQ = 8 cm, QR = 5 cm. Construct the locus of points 3 cm from Q, and the locus of points equidistant from P and R; identify the point(s) satisfying both conditions. (answer: locus 1 is a circle of radius 3 cm centred at Q; locus 2 is the angle bisector of ∠PQR, which is 45° since PQRS is a rectangle; intersection points lie where the 45° line cuts the circle)
Q18. State one real-world object that is essentially a constructed parallelogram or quadrilateral shape. (answer: not given in source — e.g. a picture frame, a tabletop, a kite)
Q19. Construct a quadrilateral ABCD given AB = 5 cm, BC = 6 cm, ∠ABC = 90°, CD = 4 cm, ∠BCD = 120°. (answer: construction task, built from two triangles via the diagonal BD)
Q20. Explain why an arbitrary quadrilateral generally requires more given information to construct than a triangle. (answer: a triangle's shape is fixed (rigid) once three suitable measurements are known — SSS, SAS, or ASA — but a quadrilateral can flex at its vertices, so one more independent measurement (typically 5 in total) is needed to pin down a unique shape)

---

### Week 8: Locus of Moving Points

**Teaching Notes**

A **locus** (plural: loci) is the set of all points that satisfy a given geometric rule — the "path" traced by a point moving under that rule.

**The four fundamental loci:**
1. **Fixed distance from a point** — the locus is a **circle** centred at that point, with radius equal to the fixed distance.
2. **Equidistant from two fixed points A and B** — the locus is the **perpendicular bisector** of the line segment AB.
3. **Fixed distance from a line** — the locus is a **pair of lines parallel** to the given line, one on each side, at that distance.
4. **Equidistant from two intersecting lines** — the locus is the **angle bisector** of the angle between them (there are two such bisectors: one for the angle and one for its supplement — interior and exterior bisectors).

To solve a locus problem, identify which of the four types applies to each condition, construct each locus, and the answer is usually where two loci intersect.

**Worked example 1:** Draw XY = 7 cm; construct the locus of points P equidistant from X and Y.
- **Step 1 — Identify the locus type:** "equidistant from two fixed points" is locus type 2 — a perpendicular bisector.
- **Step 2 — Draw the line:** using a ruler, draw XY = 7 cm.
- **Step 3 — Open the compass wide:** set the compass radius to more than half of XY (more than 3.5 cm), so the arcs from each end will actually reach across and cross each other.
- **Step 4 — Draw arcs from X:** with the compass point on X, draw one arc above the line XY and one arc below it (or one long arc that sweeps both above and below).
- **Step 5 — Draw arcs from Y (same radius, do not adjust the compass):** with the compass point on Y, draw arcs above and below the line, crossing the arcs from Step 4 at two points.
- **Step 6 — Join the two crossing points with a straight line.**
- **Step 7 — Confirm:** this line crosses XY at its exact midpoint (3.5 cm from both X and Y) and is perpendicular to it — every point on this line is equidistant from X and Y.
- **Answer: the locus is the perpendicular bisector of XY, passing through its midpoint.**

**Worked example 2 (region/area locus):** A goat is tied to post P by a 4 m rope and to post Q by a 3 m rope; P and Q are 5 m apart. Shade the region where the goat can graze.
- **Step 1 — Choose and state a scale:** since the distances are in metres and we're drawing on paper, use 1 m : 1 cm.
- **Step 2 — Draw the base line PQ to scale:** PQ = 5 cm (representing 5 m).
- **Step 3 — Identify the locus type for each rope separately:** "fixed distance from a point" is locus type 1 — a circle.
- **Step 4 — Draw the first circle:** centre P, radius 4 cm (representing the goat's 4 m rope at post P).
- **Step 5 — Draw the second circle:** centre Q, radius 3 cm (representing the goat's 3 m rope at post Q).
- **Step 6 — Identify the grazing region:** the goat can reach anywhere within 4 m of P *and* anywhere within 3 m of Q — but since the two ropes are separate, the goat's total reachable area is everywhere it can get to on *either* rope, restricted by the ropes not being able to overlap-cross physically at the same time from a single position — for the "shared/overlap" reading of the question (a point reachable by both ropes simultaneously, e.g. if tethered by both at once), shade only the lens-shaped region common to both circles.
- **Step 7 — Shade the overlapping lens-shaped region where both circles intersect.**
- **Answer: the grazing region is the lens-shaped overlap of a 4 cm circle at P and a 3 cm circle at Q, drawn on a 1 m : 1 cm scale.**

**Worked example 3:** A triangular plot ABC has BC = 105 m, ∠ABC = 45°, ∠ACB = 75° (scale 10 m : 1 cm). Construct locus l₁ of points equidistant from B and C, locus l₂ of points equidistant from AB and BC, and locus l₃ of points 5 cm from A; find T₁ and T₂, the intersections of l₁ and l₃, and measure T₁T₂.
- **Step 1 — Convert BC to the drawing scale:** 105 m at 10 m : 1 cm means BC = 105/10 = 10.5 cm on paper.
- **Step 2 — Draw BC = 10.5 cm.**
- **Step 3 — Construct 45° at B and 75° at C:** using the special-angle construction methods from Weeks 5–6 (45° = bisected 90°; 75° = 60° + 15°), draw rays from B and from C; where they meet is vertex A.
- **Step 4 — Construct l₁ (locus type 2: equidistant from B and C):** draw the perpendicular bisector of BC using equal-radius arcs from B and C.
- **Step 5 — Construct l₂ (locus type 4: equidistant from two lines AB and BC):** bisect the angle ∠ABC at vertex B.
- **Step 6 — Construct l₃ (locus type 1: fixed distance from A):** with compass centred at A, radius 5 cm, draw an arc (or full circle) — this represents points 50 m from A on the ground.
- **Step 7 — Mark T₁ and T₂ where l₁ (the perpendicular bisector of BC) crosses l₃ (the 5 cm circle centred at A)** — since a line generally crosses a circle at up to two points, label them T₁ and T₂.
- **Step 8 — Measure the straight-line distance T₁T₂ directly on the accurate drawing with a ruler**, and if needed convert back to real-world metres by multiplying by the scale factor (× 10).
- **Answer: T₁ and T₂ are the two intersection points of the perpendicular bisector of BC with the circle of radius 5 cm centred at A; T₁T₂ is measured directly from the completed, accurate construction.**

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- Memorise the four loci as a quick-reference table before any exam: (1) fixed distance from a point → circle; (2) equidistant from two points → perpendicular bisector; (3) fixed distance from a line → two parallel lines; (4) equidistant from two lines → angle bisector. Almost every locus question is just "which type is this, and where do two of them cross?"
- For "grazing goat" or "overlap region" problems, always draw both circles *fully* (not just the arcs you think you need) — the overlap region is easiest to see and shade accurately when both full circles are visible.
- Always state your scale explicitly and convert *before* you start drawing (e.g. "10 m : 1 cm") — converting mid-construction is a common source of errors; write the scaled lengths next to the real ones on your rough sketch first.
- When a problem gives you three loci and asks for an intersection, lightly sketch the whole figure freehand first to predict roughly *where* the intersection point(s) should appear — this catches gross construction errors before you commit to ink/final lines.
- To convert a measured distance back to real life, multiply by the scale's "real units per cm" (e.g. at 10 m : 1 cm, a measured 3.2 cm becomes 32 m) — always double check whether the question wants the answer in the drawing's cm or the real-world metres.

**Gamified Exercise Bank**

Q1. What is a "locus"? (answer: the set of all points satisfying a given geometric rule; the path traced by a moving point under that rule)
Q2. What is the locus of a point equidistant from two points A and B? (answer: the perpendicular bisector of AB)
Q3. What is the locus of a point equidistant from two intersecting lines? (answer: the angle bisector between them)
Q4. Describe the locus of the tip of the minute hand of a clock. (answer: a circle, with the clock's centre as centre and the hand's length as radius)
Q5. Describe, in words, the locus of a point P that always stays 5 cm from a fixed point O. (answer: a circle of radius 5 cm centred at O)
Q6. Describe the locus of points equidistant from two parallel lines l₁ and l₂ that are 6 cm apart. (answer: a straight line parallel to both, exactly 3 cm from each — midway between them)
Q7. What two tools construct the perpendicular bisector of a line? (answer: a straightedge and a pair of compasses)
Q8. What is the first step in finding the locus of points equidistant from two intersecting lines? (answer: bisect the angle formed at their intersection)
Q9. Draw XY = 7 cm; construct the locus of points P equidistant from X and Y. (answer: the perpendicular bisector of XY)
Q10. A goat is tied to post P by a 4 m rope and to post Q by a 3 m rope, with P and Q 5 m apart. Construct and shade the grazing region. (answer: the overlapping lens-shaped region of a circle radius 4 m at P and a circle radius 3 m at Q)
Q11. Draw a line AB = 75° angle by construction (60°+15°); construct a separate line CD and copy the 75° angle onto it. (answer: construction task)
Q12. Draw AB = 6 cm; construct locus l₁, the points 4 cm from line AB, and l₂, the points equidistant from A and B; mark the points P and Q where l₁ and l₂ intersect. (answer: construction task — l₁ is a pair of parallels 4 cm above and below AB; l₂ is the perpendicular bisector; P and Q are its crossings with the two parallels)
Q13. Draw two intersecting lines AB and CD forming a 45° angle; construct l₁, points equidistant from AB and CD, and l₂, points 3 cm from the intersection point; mark the point(s) satisfying both. (answer: construction task — l₁ is the 45°/135° angle bisector pair; l₂ is a circle of radius 3 cm)
Q14. Construct △ABC with AB = 8 cm, ∠A = 60°, ∠B = 45°; construct l₁, the locus of points equidistant from A and B, and l₂, the locus of points equidistant from AB and AC. Let the loci meet at P; measure AP. (answer: construction task)
Q15. Construct rectangle PQRS with PQ = 8 cm, QR = 5 cm; construct the locus of points 3 cm from Q and the locus of points equidistant from P and R; mark the point(s) satisfying both. (answer: the intersection points of a radius-3-cm circle at Q with the 45° diagonal-direction bisector of the rectangle)
Q16. A triangular plot ABC has BC = 105 m, ∠ABC = 45°, ∠ACB = 75° (scale 10 m:1 cm). Construct the loci l₁ (equidistant from B and C), l₂ (equidistant from AB and BC), and l₃ (5 cm/50 m from A). Find T₁ and T₂, the intersections of l₁ and l₃, and measure T₁T₂. (answer: construction task — answer depends on accurate drawing)
Q17. A plot of land ABC has AB = 121 m, AC = 105 m, ∠ACB = 75° (scale 10 m : 1 cm). Construct the position of a flagpole P inside the triangle that is 85 m from A and 40 m from AC, and a flagpole Q equidistant from A, B, and C. Find the distance PQ. (answer: construction task — combine a locus of points 40 m from line AC with a circle of radius 85 m centred at A for P; Q is the circumcentre, found from perpendicular bisectors of two sides)
Q18. A plot ABC has BC = 85 m and AB is given; construct the locus l₁ of points equidistant from A, C, and the locus l₂ of points 60 m from A; a tree T lies on both. Locate T and find TC in metres. (answer: construction task)
Q19. Using ruler and compasses only, construct △XYZ with XY = 10 cm, ∠XYZ = 30°, ∠YXZ = 45°; construct locus l₁ of points equidistant from Y and Z, and l₂, a line through Z parallel to XY; locate M, the intersection of l₁ and l₂; measure ∠ZMY. (answer: ∠ZMY = 120°)
Q20. A quadrilateral construction requires locating a point that is both "nearer to AC than BC" and "more than 60 m from A." Describe how you would shade this region using loci. (answer: the region on the AC-side of the angle bisector between AC and BC, intersected with the region outside a circle of radius 60 m centred at A)

### Week 9: Deductive Proofs — Parallel Lines, Congruent Triangles, Parallelogram Properties, and the Intercept Theorem

**Teaching Notes**

This week collects the essential "toolbox" of angle and shape theorems used to solve riders (multi-step geometric puzzles) and write formal proofs.

**Basic angle facts:** angles on a straight line sum to 180°; angles at a point sum to 360°; vertically opposite angles are equal.

**Parallel line theorems** (given l₁ ∥ l₂ cut by a transversal): **alternate angles are equal** ("Z-angles"); **corresponding angles are equal** ("F-angles"); **co-interior (allied) angles sum to 180°** ("C-angles").

**Triangle theorems:** angles sum to 180°; the exterior angle equals the sum of the two opposite interior angles; in an isosceles triangle, the base angles (opposite the equal sides) are equal.

**Congruent triangles** — two triangles are identical in shape and size if any one of these holds: **SSS** (all three sides equal); **SAS** (two sides and the *included* angle equal); **ASA** (two angles and the *included* side equal); **RHS** (right angle, hypotenuse, and one other side equal).

**Properties of a parallelogram:** opposite sides equal and parallel; opposite angles equal; diagonals bisect each other; adjacent angles are supplementary (sum to 180°).

**Polygon angle rules:** sum of interior angles of an n-sided polygon = (n − 2) × 180°; sum of exterior angles of any convex polygon = 360° (always).

**Intercept theorem:** if three or more parallel lines cut two transversals, they divide the transversals in the same ratio; in particular, equal intercepts on one transversal correspond to equal intercepts on the other.

**Worked example 1:** PQ ∥ RS, with a transversal cutting them so that ∠PQR = 40°; at R, a small triangle is formed with a third angle of 70° at the third vertex T; find ∠QRT.
- **Step 1 — Identify the angle relationship between the parallel lines:** since PQ ∥ RS and QR is a transversal, ∠PQR and the angle vertically or alternately linked to it at R are related by the alternate-angle rule ("Z-angles").
- **Step 2 — Apply alternate angles:** because PQ ∥ RS, the angle at R that is "alternate" to ∠PQR (on the opposite side of the transversal QR, between the parallels) is equal to 40°.
- **Step 3 — Set up the triangle QRT with this transferred angle:** the triangle formed at R now has one angle = 40° (transferred from ∠PQR by alternate angles) and another given angle = 70°.
- **Step 4 — Apply the angle-sum theorem to triangle QRT:** 40° + 70° + ∠QRT = 180°.
- **Step 5 — Add the two known angles:** 40 + 70 = 110.
- **Step 6 — Subtract from 180°:** ∠QRT = 180 − 110 = 70°.
- **Answer: ∠QRT = 70°.**

**Worked example 2:** A regular nonagon (9 sides). Find the sum of interior angles, each interior angle, and each exterior angle.
- **Step 1 — Apply the interior-angle-sum formula:** sum = (n − 2) × 180°, with n = 9.
- **Step 2 — Substitute:** sum = (9 − 2) × 180° = 7 × 180°.
- **Step 3 — Multiply:** 7 × 180 = 1260.
- **Step 4 — Since the nonagon is *regular* (all angles equal), divide the sum by the number of sides to get one interior angle:** 1260° ÷ 9 = 140°.
- **Step 5 — Find the exterior angle using the fact that all exterior angles of a convex polygon sum to 360°, and for a regular polygon each is equal:** exterior angle = 360° ÷ 9 = 40°.
- **Step 6 — Cross-check using the fact that interior and exterior angles at the same vertex are supplementary:** 140° + 40° = 180° ✓.
- **Answer: sum of interior angles = 1260°; each interior angle = 140°; each exterior angle = 40°.**

**Worked example 3 (deductive proof):** In parallelogram ABCD, diagonal AC bisects ∠A. Prove ABCD is a rhombus.
- **Step 1 — State what is given:** ABCD is a parallelogram (so AB ∥ DC and AD ∥ BC, with AB = DC and AD = BC), and diagonal AC bisects ∠DAB.
- **Step 2 — State what is to be proved:** ABCD is a rhombus, i.e. all four sides are equal (AB = BC = CD = AD).
- **Step 3 — Use the bisection condition:** since AC bisects ∠A, let ∠DAC = ∠CAB = x.
- **Step 4 — Bring in the parallel sides:** AD ∥ BC, and AC is a transversal crossing both, so ∠DAC and ∠ACB are alternate angles, meaning ∠DAC = ∠ACB. Since ∠DAC = x, we get ∠ACB = x too.
- **Step 5 — Look at triangle ABC:** it now has ∠CAB = x (from Step 3) and ∠ACB = x (from Step 4) — two equal angles.
- **Step 6 — Apply the isosceles-triangle rule (converse):** a triangle with two equal angles has the sides opposite those angles equal, so AB = BC.
- **Step 7 — Bring in the parallelogram's side property:** in any parallelogram, opposite sides are equal, so AB = DC and BC = AD.
- **Step 8 — Combine Steps 6 and 7:** since AB = BC (Step 6), and AB = DC, BC = AD (Step 7), all four sides must be equal: AB = BC = CD = AD.
- **Step 9 — Conclude:** a parallelogram with all four sides equal is, by definition, a rhombus.
- **Answer: ABCD is a rhombus. Q.E.D.**

**Worked example 4 (parallelogram angles):** Prove that opposite angles of a parallelogram are equal.
- **Step 1 — State what is given:** ABCD is a parallelogram, so AB ∥ DC and AD ∥ BC.
- **Step 2 — State what is to be proved:** ∠A = ∠C and ∠B = ∠D.
- **Step 3 — Use AB ∥ DC with AD as the transversal:** co-interior (allied) angles between parallel lines sum to 180°, so ∠A + ∠D = 180°.
- **Step 4 — Use AD ∥ BC with AB as the transversal:** again by co-interior angles, ∠A + ∠B = 180°.
- **Step 5 — Compare the two equations from Steps 3 and 4:** both equal 180°, so ∠A + ∠D = ∠A + ∠B.
- **Step 6 — Subtract ∠A from both sides:** ∠D = ∠B.
- **Step 7 — Repeat the same argument using the other pair of parallel sides (BC ∥ AD with DC as transversal, and AB ∥ DC with BC as transversal) to show ∠A + ∠B = 180° and ∠B + ∠C = 180°, giving ∠A = ∠C** by the same subtraction method.
- **Answer: ∠A = ∠C and ∠B = ∠D — opposite angles of a parallelogram are equal. Q.E.D.**

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- Memorise the "letter shapes" for spotting parallel-line angles fast: Z-shape = alternate angles (equal), F-shape = corresponding angles (equal), C/U-shape = co-interior angles (sum to 180°) — tracing the shape with your finger on the diagram is faster than recalling the rule names under exam pressure.
- For any regular polygon, you only need to remember **one** formula and derive the rest: exterior angle = 360°/n; interior angle = 180° − exterior angle; sum of interior angles = n × interior angle (or (n−2)×180°) — this avoids memorising three separate formulas.
- If a polygon question gives you the interior angle sum and asks for the number of sides, divide by 180° and add 2 immediately: n = (sum ÷ 180°) + 2 — this is faster than solving (n−2)×180° = sum as an equation from scratch.
- In deductive proofs (riders), always write the reason in brackets next to every statement (e.g. "(alternate angles, AD∥BC)") — WAEC/NECO award marks *per correct reason*, not just for the final result, so skipping reasons costs marks even with a correct conclusion.
- For congruent-triangle riders, scan the diagram for the three "free" givens first — a shared/common side, vertically opposite angles, or a midpoint (giving two equal halves) — these are the most commonly overlooked ways to complete an SSS, SAS, ASA, or RHS argument.

**Gamified Exercise Bank**

Q1. List the three main parallel-line angle theorems. (answer: alternate angles equal, corresponding angles equal, co-interior angles sum to 180°)
Q2. What are the four conditions for proving triangles congruent? (answer: SSS, SAS, ASA, RHS)
Q3. What is the formula for the sum of interior angles of an n-sided polygon? (answer: (n − 2) × 180°)
Q4. What is the sum of all exterior angles of any polygon? (answer: 360°)
Q5. What is the difference between a parallelogram and a rhombus? (answer: a rhombus is a parallelogram with all four sides equal, not just opposite sides)
Q6. In parallelogram ABCD, ∠A = 100°. Find ∠B, ∠C, ∠D. (answer: ∠B = 80°, ∠C = 100°, ∠D = 80°)
Q7. What does the Intercept Theorem state? (answer: parallel lines cutting two transversals divide them in the same ratio — equal intercepts on one give equal intercepts on the other)
Q8. State the SAS condition for congruence. Why must the angle be "included"? (answer: two sides and the angle between them equal; if the angle isn't between the two given sides, the triangles need not be congruent — the shape isn't pinned down uniquely)
Q9. In the diagram PQ ∥ RS, ∠PQR = 40°, and a triangle at R has another angle 70°; find ∠QRT. (answer: 70°)
Q10. A regular polygon has 9 sides (nonagon). Find (a) the sum of interior angles, (b) each interior angle, (c) each exterior angle. (answer: (a) 1260°; (b) 140°; (c) 40°)
Q11. A regular polygon has 10 sides. Find (i) its exterior angle, (ii) its interior angle. (answer: (i) 36°; (ii) 144°)
Q12. Given a parallelogram ABCD where diagonal AC bisects ∠A, prove ABCD is a rhombus. (answer: see Teaching Notes — via alternate angles and isosceles triangle reasoning)
Q13. Prove that opposite angles of a parallelogram are equal. (answer: see Teaching Notes — via co-interior angles on both pairs of parallel sides)
Q14. A polygon has an interior angle sum of 900°. How many sides does it have? (answer: 7 sides)
Q15. The exterior angle of a regular polygon is 30°. How many sides does it have? (answer: 12 sides)
Q16. Given an isosceles triangle ABC with AB = AC, and M the midpoint of BC, prove △ABM ≅ △ACM. Which condition is used? (answer: SSS — AB=AC given, BM=CM since M is the midpoint, and AM is common — or SAS using the equal base angles and AM common)
Q17. Find the interior angle of a regular pentagon (5 sides). (answer: 108°)
Q18. In the diagram, AB is a straight line; two angles marked 3x and 2x lie on it with a third angle 50°. Find x. (answer: not given in source — depends on the specific diagram; using 3x+2x+50=180 gives x=26°)
Q19. Find x, y, z in a diagram of intersecting lines and a triangle, where vertically opposite, co-interior, and triangle angle-sum rules must all be combined. (answer: not given in source — solve by chaining the listed angle rules step by step, giving a reason at each stage)
Q20. Two triangles share a common side and have two pairs of equal angles; state which congruence condition applies and why. (answer: ASA (or AAS) — if the shared/given side lies between the two equal angles it is ASA; if not, it is AAS, which is still sufficient since the third angles must also then be equal)
Q21. A quadrilateral's angles are (x+10)°, 2x°, 90°, and (x+20)°. Find x. (answer: not given in source — sum of quadrilateral angles is 360°, so (x+10)+2x+90+(x+20)=360, giving 4x+120=360, x=60°)
Q22. State why the sum of the exterior angles of any convex polygon is always 360°, regardless of the number of sides. (answer: because walking once fully around the polygon's boundary means turning through one complete revolution — 360° — in total, one exterior-angle turn at each vertex)

### Week 10: Statistics I — Data Collection, Tabulation, and Range, Median, Mode of Ungrouped and Grouped Data

**Teaching Notes**

**Statistics** is the science of collecting, organising, analysing, and interpreting data. The **population** is the entire group of interest; a **sample** is a smaller, representative subgroup studied when the population is too large. **Primary data** is collected first-hand (questionnaires, interviews, experiments, observation); **secondary data** is obtained from an existing source (reports, textbooks, databases).

**Types of data:** **qualitative (categorical)** data describes a quality, not a number (e.g. favourite colour); **quantitative (numerical)** data is either **discrete** (countable, e.g. number of children) or **continuous** (measurable, can take any value in a range, e.g. height).

**Raw data** is unorganised, as collected. An **array** arranges it in ascending or descending order. A **frequency table** tallies how many times each value occurs.

**Measures of central tendency (location):**
- **Mean** (x̄): x̄ = Σx / n for raw data; for a frequency distribution, x̄ = Σfx / Σf.
- **Median:** arrange the data in an array (ascending order); if n is odd, the median is the middle value; if n is even, it is the mean of the two middle values.
- **Mode:** the value that occurs with the greatest frequency (there may be more than one mode, or none).

**Range** (a simple measure of spread): the difference between the largest and smallest values in the data.

**Grouped data:** raw data is organised into **class intervals** (e.g. 40–42, 43–45, …). **Class boundaries** close the gaps between stated class limits (subtract 0.5 from the lower limit, add 0.5 to the upper limit, or average the gap between adjacent classes). **Class width** = upper boundary − lower boundary. **Class mark (midpoint)** = (lower limit + upper limit)/2.

**Median of grouped data:** first locate the median class using cumulative frequency (the class containing position n/2, or Σf/2), then apply:

**Median = L₁ + [(n/2 − Cfb)/Fm] × C**

where L₁ = lower class boundary of the median class, n = total frequency, Cfb = cumulative frequency before the median class, Fm = frequency of the median class, C = class width.

**Mode of grouped data:**

**Mode = L₁ + [Δ₁/(Δ₁ + Δ₂)] × C**

where L₁ = lower boundary of the modal class (the class with the highest frequency), Δ₁ = (modal frequency − frequency of class before it), Δ₂ = (modal frequency − frequency of class after it), C = class width.

**Worked example 1:** Find the mean of 1, 3, 4, 8, 8, 4, 7.
- **Step 1 — Recall the mean formula for raw data:** mean = Σx / n (sum of all values, divided by how many values there are).
- **Step 2 — Add up all the values:** 1 + 3 + 4 + 8 + 8 + 4 + 7.
- **Step 3 — Add carefully in pairs to avoid mistakes:** 1+3=4; 4+4=8; 8+8=16; 16+8=24; 24+4=28; 28+7=35.
- **Step 4 — Count how many values there are:** n = 7.
- **Step 5 — Divide the sum by n:** mean = 35 ÷ 7 = 5.
- **Answer: mean = 5.**

**Worked example 2:** Find the median of 6, 1, 0, 3, 10, 2, 5.
- **Step 1 — Recall that the median needs the data sorted first — arrange the values into an array (ascending order).**
- **Step 2 — Sort the numbers:** 0, 1, 2, 3, 5, 6, 10.
- **Step 3 — Count the values:** n = 7 (an odd number).
- **Step 4 — Since n is odd, the median is the single middle value — find its position using (n+1)/2:** position = (7+1)/2 = 4th value.
- **Step 5 — Count to the 4th value in the sorted array (0, 1, 2, [3], 5, 6, 10):** the 4th value is 3.
- **Answer: median = 3.**

**Worked example 3:** Find the median of 5, 7, 11, 9, 15, 12, 18, 6.
- **Step 1 — Sort the numbers into an array:** 5, 6, 7, 9, 11, 12, 15, 18.
- **Step 2 — Count the values:** n = 8 (an even number).
- **Step 3 — Since n is even, the median is the mean (average) of the two middle values — find their positions using n/2 and (n/2)+1:** positions = 4th and 5th values.
- **Step 4 — Identify the 4th and 5th values in the array (5, 6, 7, [9], [11], 12, 15, 18):** they are 9 and 11.
- **Step 5 — Average them:** median = (9 + 11)/2 = 20/2 = 10.
- **Answer: median = 10.**

**Worked example 4:** For the frequency table Scores 1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8 with frequencies 3,4,3,6,5,4,3,2, find the mean.
- **Step 1 — Recall the formula for the mean of a frequency distribution:** mean = Σfx / Σf (sum of each score × its frequency, divided by the total frequency).
- **Step 2 — Build an fx column by multiplying each score by its frequency:** 1×3=3; 2×4=8; 3×3=9; 4×6=24; 5×5=25; 6×4=24; 7×3=21; 8×2=16.
- **Step 3 — Add up the frequency column (Σf):** 3+4+3+6+5+4+3+2 = 30.
- **Step 4 — Add up the fx column (Σfx):** 3+8+9+24+25+24+21+16 = 130.
- **Step 5 — Divide Σfx by Σf:** mean = 130 ÷ 30.
- **Step 6 — Simplify/evaluate:** 130 ÷ 30 = 4.333… ≈ 4.3 (1 d.p.).
- **Answer: mean ≈ 4.3.**

**Worked example 5 (grouped median):** A distribution has median class 21–25 with class boundaries 20.5–25.5, cumulative frequency before this class Cfb = 16, frequency of this class Fm = 9, class width C = 5, n = 50.
- **Step 1 — Recall the grouped-median formula:** Median = L₁ + [(n/2 − Cfb)/Fm] × C.
- **Step 2 — Identify each symbol from the question:** L₁ = 20.5 (lower boundary of median class), n = 50, Cfb = 16, Fm = 9, C = 5.
- **Step 3 — Compute n/2:** 50/2 = 25.
- **Step 4 — Subtract Cfb from n/2:** 25 − 16 = 9.
- **Step 5 — Divide by Fm:** 9 ÷ 9 = 1.
- **Step 6 — Multiply by the class width C:** 1 × 5 = 5.
- **Step 7 — Add this to L₁:** Median = 20.5 + 5.
- **Answer: median = 25.5.**

**Worked example 6 (grouped mode):** The modal class is 26–30 (boundaries 25.5–30.5); the class before it has frequency 9, the modal class has frequency 13, the class after it has frequency 8; class width C = 5.
- **Step 1 — Recall the grouped-mode formula:** Mode = L₁ + [Δ₁/(Δ₁ + Δ₂)] × C.
- **Step 2 — Identify L₁:** the lower class boundary of the modal class = 25.5.
- **Step 3 — Compute Δ₁ (modal frequency minus the frequency of the class *before* it):** Δ₁ = 13 − 9 = 4.
- **Step 4 — Compute Δ₂ (modal frequency minus the frequency of the class *after* it):** Δ₂ = 13 − 8 = 5.
- **Step 5 — Add Δ₁ and Δ₂:** 4 + 5 = 9.
- **Step 6 — Divide Δ₁ by this sum:** 4 ÷ 9 ≈ 0.444.
- **Step 7 — Multiply by the class width C:** 0.444 × 5 ≈ 2.22.
- **Step 8 — Add this to L₁:** Mode = 25.5 + 2.22.
- **Answer: mode ≈ 27.72.**

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- For the median's position, use "odd → (n+1)/2-th value" and "even → average the (n/2)-th and (n/2 + 1)-th values" as a fixed rule — don't try to "eyeball" the middle of a long list; always sort fully into an array first, since an unsorted list gives a wrong median even with correct counting.
- When finding the mean from a frequency table, build the fx column directly beside the frequency column and total *both* columns before dividing — never divide by the number of *distinct* scores (8, in Worked Example 4) instead of the total frequency Σf (30) — this is the single most common error in grouped-mean questions.
- In Δ₁/Δ₂ mode calculations, remember Δ₁ always compares the modal class to the class **before** it, and Δ₂ to the class **after** it — reversing them shifts the mode toward the wrong neighbouring class.
- A fast built-in check for any median-of-grouped-data answer: it must fall *within* the median class's boundaries (e.g. between 20.5 and 25.5 in Worked Example 5) — if your computed median falls outside that range, you've made an arithmetic error.
- To classify data quickly under time pressure, ask two questions in sequence: "Can I count it in whole numbers?" (yes → discrete; no → continuous, if numerical) and "Is it even a number at all?" (no → qualitative).

**Gamified Exercise Bank**

Q1. Define Statistics. (answer: the science of collecting, organising, analysing and interpreting data)
Q2. What is the difference between Primary and Secondary data? (answer: primary data is collected first-hand by the researcher; secondary data is already collected by someone else)
Q3. What is the difference between Qualitative and Quantitative data? (answer: qualitative data describes categories/qualities (non-numerical); quantitative data consists of numbers)
Q4. Give an example each of Discrete and Continuous data. (answer: discrete — number of siblings; continuous — height)
Q5. What is the purpose of the Tally column in a frequency table? (answer: a quick way to count occurrences while going through the raw data, before totalling as the frequency)
Q6. A researcher gets data from the National Bureau of Statistics. Is this primary or secondary data? (answer: secondary data)
Q7. A student personally counts cars passing the school gate. Is this primary or secondary data? (answer: primary data)
Q8. Classify: (a) phone brands in a class, (b) number of siblings, (c) time to run 100 m. (answer: (a) qualitative; (b) discrete; (c) continuous)
Q9. What is "raw data"? (answer: data as collected, not yet organised or arranged in order)
Q10. Find the mean of 1, 3, 4, 8, 8, 4, 7. (answer: 5)
Q11. Calculate the mean of 1, 0, 1, 2, 3, 2, 0, 2, 3, 2. (answer: 1.6)
Q12. Calculate the mean of 4, 3, 6, 8, 9, 3, 9. (answer: 6)
Q13. Divide the sum of 8, 6, 7, 2, 0, 4, 7, 2, 3 by their mean. (answer: 9, since sum/mean = n = 9)
Q14. If the mean of 6, 8, 9, x, 5, 7 is 8, find x. (answer: 13)
Q15. The weights of 5 girls are 48, x, 52, 50, and (2x−5) kg; the average is 47 kg. Find the heaviest girl's weight. (answer: 55 kg)
Q16. The ages of Abu, Segun, Kofi, Funmi are 17, (2x−13), 14, and 16 years; their mean age is 17.5. Find x. (answer: 18)
Q17. The average cost of 20 articles is ₦213; the average cost of the first 9 is ₦115. Find the average cost of the remaining 11 articles. (answer: ≈₦293.18)
Q18. The mean age of 10 students is 10 years 6 months. Removing two students aged 12y10m and 13y6m, find the mean age of the remaining 8. (answer: ≈9 years 10 months)
Q19. The mean of 68, 65, x, 69, 77, 48, 64 is 67. Find x. (answer: 78)
Q20. The table shows scores 1–8 with frequencies 3,4,3,6,5,4,3,2. Find the mean. (answer: ≈4.3)
Q21. Twenty girls and y boys sat an exam; girls' mean = 62, boys' mean = 57; total score = 2950. Find y. (answer: 30)
Q22. The average of 10 boys was 12 years; a 15-year-old boy is replaced by a 5-year-old. Find the new average. (answer: 11 years)
Q23. For the distribution: Scores 2,4,5,6,7 with frequencies 5,3,6,4,2, find the mean. (answer: 4.5)
Q24. The mean of a frequency distribution (x-values 0, p, 3 with frequencies 2, 3, 1) is 1.5. Find p. (answer: 2)
Q25. A frequency table (scores 1–6, frequencies 1,4,x,6,2,2) has mean 3.5. Find x. (answer: 5)
Q26. Find the median of 6, 1, 0, 3, 10, 2, 5. (answer: 3)
Q27. Find the median of 5, 7, 11, 9, 15, 12, 18, 6. (answer: 10)
Q28. Find the median of 2.64, 2.50, 2.72, 2.91, 2.35. (answer: 2.64)
Q29. Calculate the median of 3, 2, 6, 8, 9, 6, 8, 12, 11, 12. (answer: 8)
Q30. What is the median of 22, 41, 35, 63, 82, 74? (answer: 52)
Q31. Find the median of 16, 20, 8, 14, 12, 16, 10, 12, 7, 16. (answer: 13, midpoint of the 5th and 6th ordered values)
Q32. Calculate the median of 6, 8, 3, 2, 10, 6, 11, 9, 12, 4. (answer: not given in source — array: 2,3,4,6,6,8,9,10,11,12; median = (6+8)/2 = 7)
Q33. What is the mode of: 1,2,3,4,4,5,5,5,4,2,2,3,4,5,5,6? (answer: 5)
Q34. Ages of pupils: 7,9,6,10,8,8,9,11,8,7,9,6,8,9,10,7,8,7,9,8. Find the mode. (answer: 8)
Q35. A type of average that shows the most popular item is called what? (answer: the mode)
Q36. For a distribution with x = 2,6,10 and f = 5,10,9, find the mode. (answer: 6)
Q37. The ages of 10 students are 15,16,15.5,17,14.9,14.5,14.1,15.1,15.2,14.8. Find the range. (answer: 2.9)
Q38. Numbers in ascending order (x−2), 8, (5+x), 12, (x+14). Find the range. (answer: 16)
Q39. For x = 1,2,3,5,6,7,8 with frequencies 3,4,5,7,6,5,4, find the range of ages. (answer: 7)
Q40. Which of the following is NOT a measure of dispersion: interquartile range, mean, mean deviation, range, standard deviation? (answer: mean)
Q41. State the formula for the lower quartile Q1, middle quartile Q2, and upper quartile Q3 in terms of cumulative frequency. (answer: Q1 = ¼(cum. freq.), Q2 = 2/4(cum. freq.), Q3 = ¾(cum. freq.))
Q42. State the formula for the semi-interquartile range. (answer: (Q3 − Q1)/2)
Q43. Marks: 6,7,8,12,15,8,9,5,28,15,17,21. Find the semi-interquartile range. (answer: 4)
Q44. Shoe sizes of 12 students: size 4,6,7,9,14 with frequencies 3,2,4,2,1. Find the semi-interquartile range. (answer: 1.5)
Q45. What is the median class formula for grouped data when Σf is even? (answer: median class position = (Σf)/2)
Q46. What is the median class formula for grouped data when Σf is odd? (answer: median class position = (Σf + 1)/2)
Q47. A group of families reports numbers of children: 1,2,3,4,5,6 with frequencies 10,15,8,6,4,7. Find the median. (answer: 2)
Q48. Daily wages (₦100s): 2,3,4,5,6,8,10 with frequencies 2,4,10,11,15,10,3. Find (a) total workers, (b) the median wage. (answer: (a) 55; (b) ₦600)
Q49. Scores 1–6 with frequencies 2,5,x,11,9,10 (x unknown); probability of scoring 3 is 0.26. Find x, then the median. (answer: x = 13; median = 4)
Q50. State the formula for the median of grouped data, defining every symbol. (answer: Median = L₁ + [(n/2 − Cfb)/Fm] × C, where L₁ = lower boundary of median class, n = total frequency, Cfb = cumulative frequency before the median class, Fm = frequency of the median class, C = class width)
Q51. State the formula for the mode of grouped data, defining every symbol. (answer: Mode = L₁ + [Δ₁/(Δ₁+Δ₂)] × C, where L₁ = lower boundary of the modal class, Δ₁ = modal frequency minus the frequency of the class before it, Δ₂ = modal frequency minus the frequency of the class after it, C = class width)
Q52. A frequency table has modal class 41–50 (boundaries 40.5–50.5), with the class before it having frequency 90, the modal class 110, and the class after it 70; class width 10. Find the mode. (answer: 43.83)
Q53. Weights of 50 students (raw data, 65…59) are grouped into class intervals 45–49, 50–54, … Calculate the mean weight using assumed-mean or direct methods once tabulated. (answer: 35, using the direct method with midpoints)
Q54. State the two formulas for the mean of grouped data (with and without an assumed mean). (answer: x̄ = Σfx/Σf (direct); x̄ = A + Σfd/Σf, where A is the assumed mean and d = x − A (assumed-mean method))

---

### Week 11: Statistics II — Statistical Graphs (Bar Chart, Pie Chart, Histogram, Cumulative Frequency Curve); Mean Deviation, Variance and Standard Deviation

**Teaching Notes**

**Bar chart:** used for discrete or qualitative data; bars have gaps between them, same width, and can be rearranged; the x-axis lists categories or discrete values.

**Histogram:** used for continuous, grouped data; bars must touch (no gaps); the x-axis is marked with class boundaries.

**Pie chart:** a circle divided into sectors, each representing a proportion of the whole (360° = the total frequency, or 100%):

**Angle of a sector = (frequency of item / total frequency) × 360°**

**Frequency polygon:** a line graph joining the midpoints of the tops of histogram bars, "grounded" by adding an imaginary zero-frequency class at each end so the shape closes.

**Cumulative frequency curve (ogive):** plots the *upper class boundary* of each class against its *cumulative frequency*. Used to read off the median (the value at Σf/2), quartiles, deciles, and percentiles directly from the curve.
- Lower quartile Q1 = ¼ of the cumulative frequency; upper quartile Q3 = ¾ of the cumulative frequency.
- Inter-quartile range = Q3 − Q1; semi-inter-quartile range = (Q3 − Q1)/2.

**Range:** largest value − smallest value (the simplest, but weakest, measure of spread, since it only uses two extreme values).

**Mean deviation (M.D.):** the average absolute distance of each value from the mean:

**M.D. = Σ|x − x̄| / n** (ungrouped); **M.D. = Σf|x − x̄| / Σf** (grouped/frequency distribution)

**Variance and standard deviation** measure how spread out the data is around the mean:

**Variance V = Σ(x − x̄)² / n** (ungrouped); **V = Σf(x − x̄)² / Σf** (grouped)

**Standard deviation S.D. = √Variance**

A useful computational shortcut for grouped data: **S.D. = √[ Σfx²/Σf − (Σfx/Σf)² ]**

**Worked example 1:** Find the variance and standard deviation of 2, 5, 6, 3, 4.
- **Step 1 — Find the mean:** x̄ = (2+5+6+3+4)/5 = 20/5 = 4.
- **Step 2 — Find each deviation from the mean (x − x̄):** 2−4=−2; 5−4=1; 6−4=2; 3−4=−1; 4−4=0.
- **Step 3 — Square each deviation (this removes the negative signs and is why variance is always ≥ 0):** (−2)²=4; (1)²=1; (2)²=4; (−1)²=1; (0)²=0.
- **Step 4 — Sum the squared deviations:** 4+1+4+1+0 = 10.
- **Step 5 — Divide by n to get the variance:** Variance = 10/5 = 2.
- **Step 6 — Take the square root of the variance to get the standard deviation:** S.D. = √2.
- **Step 7 — Evaluate:** √2 ≈ 1.4 (2 s.f.).
- **Answer: Variance = 2; Standard deviation ≈ 1.4.**

**Worked example 2 (mean deviation):** Find the mean deviation of 2, 4, 6, 5, 3.
- **Step 1 — Find the mean:** x̄ = (2+4+6+5+3)/5 = 20/5 = 4.
- **Step 2 — Find each deviation from the mean, then take its absolute value (ignore the sign):** |2−4|=2; |4−4|=0; |6−4|=2; |5−4|=1; |3−4|=1.
- **Step 3 — Sum the absolute deviations:** 2+0+2+1+1 = 6.
- **Step 4 — Divide by n:** M.D. = 6/5.
- **Step 5 — Evaluate:** 6 ÷ 5 = 1.2.
- **Answer: mean deviation = 1.2.**

**Worked example 3 (pie chart):** An SS1 class of 80 students has 30 in Arts, 40 in Science, 10 in Commercial. Find each sector's angle.
- **Step 1 — Recall the pie-chart angle formula:** angle = (frequency of item / total frequency) × 360°.
- **Step 2 — Confirm the total:** 30 + 40 + 10 = 80 students ✓ (matches the given total).
- **Step 3 — Find the Arts angle:** (30/80) × 360°.
- **Step 4 — Simplify 30/80 = 3/8 first:** (3/8) × 360° = 3 × 45° = 135°.
- **Step 5 — Find the Science angle:** (40/80) × 360°.
- **Step 6 — Simplify 40/80 = 1/2:** (1/2) × 360° = 180°.
- **Step 7 — Find the Commercial angle:** (10/80) × 360°.
- **Step 8 — Simplify 10/80 = 1/8:** (1/8) × 360° = 45°.
- **Step 9 — Check that all sector angles add to exactly 360°:** 135° + 180° + 45° = 360° ✓.
- **Answer: Arts = 135°, Science = 180°, Commercial = 45°.**

**Worked example 4 (cumulative frequency / median from an ogive):** For a distribution with total frequency n = 200, find the median position and describe how to read the median mark from the ogive.
- **Step 1 — Recall that the median corresponds to the cumulative-frequency value at position n/2 on a cumulative frequency curve.**
- **Step 2 — Compute n/2:** 200/2 = 100.
- **Step 3 — On the cumulative-frequency (vertical) axis, locate the value 100.**
- **Step 4 — Draw a horizontal line from 100 across until it meets the ogive curve.**
- **Step 5 — From that meeting point on the curve, draw a vertical line straight down to the marks (horizontal) axis.**
- **Step 6 — Read off the value where this vertical line meets the marks axis — this is the estimated median mark** (for example, ≈58.0, though the exact value depends on the specific curve drawn from the data).
- **Answer: median position = 100th value; the median mark itself is read directly off the ogive at that cumulative-frequency height (e.g. ≈58.0 for a typical such curve).**

**Worked example 5 (standard deviation, grouped):** For marks 51–60, 61–70, 71–80, 81–90, 91–100 with frequencies 11, 23, 39, 17, 10 (Σf = 100), find the mean, mean deviation, and standard deviation.
- **Step 1 — Find the midpoint (class mark) of each class interval:** 51–60 → 55.5; 61–70 → 65.5; 71–80 → 75.5; 81–90 → 85.5; 91–100 → 95.5.
- **Step 2 — Multiply each midpoint by its frequency (fx):** 55.5×11=610.5; 65.5×23=1506.5; 75.5×39=2944.5; 85.5×17=1453.5; 95.5×10=955.
- **Step 3 — Sum the fx column:** 610.5+1506.5+2944.5+1453.5+955 = 7470.
- **Step 4 — Divide by Σf = 100 to get the mean:** mean = 7470/100 = 74.7.
- **Step 5 — For the mean deviation, find |x − x̄| for each midpoint (x̄ = 74.7):** |55.5−74.7|=19.2; |65.5−74.7|=9.2; |75.5−74.7|=0.8; |85.5−74.7|=10.8; |95.5−74.7|=20.8.
- **Step 6 — Multiply each by its frequency (f|x−x̄|):** 19.2×11=211.2; 9.2×23=211.6; 0.8×39=31.2; 10.8×17=183.6; 20.8×10=208.
- **Step 7 — Sum this column:** 211.2+211.6+31.2+183.6+208 = 845.6.
- **Step 8 — Divide by Σf = 100:** M.D. = 845.6/100 = 8.456 ≈ 8 (nearest whole number).
- **Step 9 — For the standard deviation, use the shortcut formula S.D. = √[Σfx²/Σf − (Σfx/Σf)²]; first find fx² for each class (fx × x):** 610.5×55.5≈33 882.75; 1506.5×65.5≈98 675.75; 2944.5×75.5≈222 309.75; 1453.5×85.5≈124 274.25; 955×95.5≈91 202.5.
- **Step 10 — Sum Σfx²:** 33 882.75+98 675.75+222 309.75+124 274.25+91 202.5 = 570 345.
- **Step 11 — Divide by Σf:** 570 345/100 = 5703.45.
- **Step 12 — Subtract the mean squared:** (74.7)² = 5580.09; 5703.45 − 5580.09 = 123.36.
- **Step 13 — Take the square root:** √123.36 ≈ 11.11 ≈ 11 (nearest whole number).
- **Answer: mean = 74.7 (≈75 to the nearest whole number); mean deviation ≈ 8; standard deviation ≈ 11.**

**⚡ Shortcut & Speed Tips**
- For variance/S.D., always compute the mean first, freeze it, then build a simple deviation table with columns x, (x−x̄), (x−x̄)² — laying out a table (rather than doing it all "in your head") is what prevents sign errors from the negative deviations.
- Remember variance and standard deviation are always non-negative, and S.D. = √Variance — so if you're given the variance, taking the square root is a one-line answer, no need to redo the whole calculation from raw data.
- For pie charts, simplify each frequency-to-total fraction to its lowest terms *before* multiplying by 360° (e.g. 30/80 → 3/8) — smaller numbers are much faster to multiply mentally, and 360° divides evenly by many small denominators (2,3,4,5,6,8,9,10,12...).
- Always finish a pie-chart question by checking that all sector angles sum to exactly 360° — this single check catches almost every arithmetic slip in the whole question.
- For grouped standard deviation, the shortcut formula S.D. = √[Σfx²/Σf − (Σfx/Σf)²] avoids computing every individual (x−x̄)² by hand — build an fx² column alongside your usual fx column from the start, since you need both Σfx (for the mean) and Σfx² (for the shortcut) anyway.
- On an ogive, the median is always read at the n/2 mark on the cumulative-frequency axis, the lower quartile at n/4, and the upper quartile at 3n/4 — memorising these three fractions (¼, ½, ¾) lets you answer several ogive sub-questions from a single drawn curve without re-plotting anything.

**Gamified Exercise Bank**

Q1. Why do we use "grouped" frequency tables for large or continuous datasets? (answer: to organise many individual values into manageable class intervals, making patterns easier to see)
Q2. What is the main physical difference between a bar chart and a histogram? (answer: bar chart bars have gaps; histogram bars touch)
Q3. What type of data is a bar chart used for? (answer: discrete or qualitative data)
Q4. What type of data is a histogram used for? (answer: continuous, grouped data)
Q5. What is plotted on the x-axis of a histogram? (answer: class boundaries)
Q6. For class interval 20–29, find (a) the class midpoint, (b) the class boundaries. (answer: (a) 24.5; (b) 19.5–29.5)
Q7. If you have data for "number of children per family," would you use a bar chart or a histogram? Explain. (answer: bar chart — it is discrete, countable data)
Q8. If you have data for "height of students," would you use a bar chart or a histogram? Explain. (answer: histogram — it is continuous, measured data)
Q9. What is the main purpose of a pie chart? (answer: to show how a total is divided into proportional parts)
Q10. State the formula for the angle of a sector in a pie chart. (answer: angle = (frequency of item/total frequency) × 360°)
Q11. In an SS1 class of 80 students, 30 study Arts, 40 Science, 10 Commercial. Find the angle for each stream on a pie chart. (answer: Arts 135°, Science 180°, Commercial 45°)
Q12. A sector on a pie chart represents 20 people out of 80. Find its angle. (answer: 90°)
Q13. A pie chart shows "Food" taking up 120° of a student's budget. What percentage is this? (answer: 33⅓%)
Q14. What is plotted on the x-axis of a frequency polygon? (answer: class midpoints)
Q15. Why must a frequency polygon be "grounded" at zero frequency at both ends? (answer: so it forms a closed shape (polygon) that starts and ends on the baseline, rather than an open line)
Q16. What is the main advantage of a frequency polygon over a histogram? (answer: it more clearly shows the overall shape/trend of the distribution, and allows easy comparison of two datasets on one graph)
Q17. What does a cumulative frequency curve (ogive) plot? (answer: upper class boundaries against cumulative frequency)
Q18. What can a cumulative frequency curve be used to estimate? (answer: the median, quartiles, deciles, and percentiles)
Q19. State the formula for the lower quartile Q1 and upper quartile Q3 using cumulative frequency. (answer: Q1 = ¼ × cumulative frequency; Q3 = ¾ × cumulative frequency)
Q20. The table shows marks 11–20,…,91–100 for 200 candidates with cumulative frequencies building to 200; use the ogive to estimate (i) the interquartile range, (ii) the minimum pass mark for the top 5%. (answer: (i) 24; (ii) 84.5)
Q21. From an ogive for 80 students (marks 1–10,…,91–100), find (c)(i) the median mark, (ii) the lower quartile. (answer: (i) 45.5; (ii) 31.5)
Q22. From the same ogive, find the probability that a randomly picked student (i) scored 75% or above, (ii) failed at a 40% pass mark. (answer: (i) 77/80; (ii) 3/8)
Q23. For 200 candidates (marks 0–9,…,90–99), use the ogive to estimate (i) the lowest mark among the top 5% (distinction), (ii) the probability of scoring at most 45%. (answer: (i) 85.5; (ii) 7/20)
Q24. This is the simplest measure of spread; define it, and note its main weakness. (answer: range = largest − smallest value; weakness — it only uses two extreme values, ignoring the rest of the data)
Q25. Find the mean deviation of 2, 4, 6, 5, 3. (answer: 1.2)
Q26. Calculate the mean deviation of 0, −1, −3, 4, 5, 1. (answer: 7/3)
Q27. Find the mean deviation of 20, 25, 21, 27, 28, 29, to the nearest whole number. (answer: 3)
Q28. Calculate the mean deviation of 6, 7, 8, 9, 10. (answer: 1.2)
Q29. Find the mean deviation of 2, 3, 5, 6. (answer: 1.5)
Q30. A die is thrown 50 times; scores 1–6 have frequencies 2,5,13,11,9,10. Calculate the mean deviation. (answer: 1.16)
Q31. State the formula for variance of a set of n raw numbers. (answer: V = Σ(x−x̄)²/n)
Q32. State the formula for standard deviation. (answer: S.D. = √Variance = √[Σ(x−x̄)²/n])
Q33. If the variance of a distribution is 25, find the standard deviation. (answer: 5)
Q34. Find the variance and standard deviation of 2, 5, 6, 3, 4. (answer: variance = 2; S.D. ≈ 1.4)
Q35. Calculate the variance of 5, 11, 13, 14, 17. (answer: 16.0)
Q36. Given the mean of 15, 21, 17, 26, 18, 29 is 21, find the standard deviation. (answer: 5)
Q37. Find the standard deviation of 6, 0, 4, 3, 2. (answer: 2)
Q38. Calculate the standard deviation of 2, 3, 4, 4, 5, 6. (answer: 1.29)
Q39. A distribution (x = 1,2,3,4,5 with f = 2,1,2,1,2) — find the variance and standard deviation. (answer: variance = 2.25; S.D. = 1.5)
Q40. For scores 1–6 with frequencies 2,5,x,11,9,10 and probability of a 3 being 0.26, find (a) x, (b) the standard deviation. (answer: (a) x = 13; (b) S.D. ≈ 1.51, from Σf(x−x̄)²/Σf with mean 4)
Q41. A table (marks 51–60,…,91–100 with frequencies 11,23,39,17,10) has total 100 students. Find (i) the mean mark, (ii) the mean deviation, (iii) the standard deviation, all to the nearest whole number. (answer: (i) 75; (ii) 8; (iii) 11)
Q42. State the shortcut formula for standard deviation of grouped data using Σfx² and Σfx. (answer: S.D. = √[Σfx²/Σf − (Σfx/Σf)²])
Q43. What is the mean deviation of a grouped frequency distribution, in formula terms? (answer: M.D. = Σf|x − x̄| / Σf)
Q44. If the mean and median of the five numbers 170, 230, y, 215, 235 are 210 and x respectively, find x and y. (answer: x = 215, y = 200)
Q45. The mean height of 100 students (grouped, 1.40–1.63 m) is found via the assumed-mean method; state which columns must be prepared to compute both the mean and the mean deviation. (answer: x (midpoint), f, fx or fd, |x−x̄|, f|x−x̄|)
Q46. Draw a histogram for marks 1–5,6–10,…,21–25 with frequencies 4,6,11,8,1; use it to estimate the mode, and state the probability a randomly selected student scored at most 15 marks. (answer: probability = (4+6+11)/30 = 21/30 = 7/10; modal class 11–15)
Q47. Why is standard deviation generally preferred over mean deviation as a measure of spread in advanced statistics? (answer: not given in source — squaring removes the need for absolute values and gives more mathematical weight to larger deviations, and it connects directly to variance used in further statistical theory)
Q48. A worker in a factory earns wages recorded across 50 workers grouped into class intervals 21–30, 31–40, etc. Outline the steps to draw the cumulative frequency curve and use it to find the median and inter-quartile range. (answer: construct the cumulative frequency table with upper class boundaries, plot boundary vs. cumulative frequency, draw a smooth curve, then read off Σf/2 for the median and the ¼/¾ points for the quartiles)

- Second Term: 327 exercise/gamified questions (Weeks 1–11, restarting Q1 each week).
- Third Term: 305 exercise/gamified questions (Weeks 1–11, restarting Q1 each week).
